Preface 
This Report for the period ended March 2024 has been prepared for 
submission to the Governor of Madhya Pradesh under Article 151 of 
the Constitution of India for being laid before the Legislature of the 
State. 
This Report contains significant findings of Compliance Audits of 
Higher Education Department, Panchayat and Rural Development 
Department and Public Health and Medical Education Department of 
Madhya Pradesh.  
The instances mentioned in the Report are those which came to notice 
in the course of test audit during the period 2024 -25. The instances 
which came to notice in earlier years, but could not be reported in 
previous Audit Reports have also been included. Fu rther, instances 
relating to the period subsequent to 2024-25 have also been included, 
wherever necessary. 
The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing 
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India. 

 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
Chapter I 
 
Overview 

Chapter-I: Overview 
 
1 | P a g e  
Chapter-I: Overview 
1.1 About this Report  
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG) contains matters arising 
from Compliance Audit of various auditee departments of the Government of Madhya 
Pradesh. 
The primary purpose of this Report is to bring to the notice of the State Legislature, 
significant results of audit. The findings of audit are expected to enable the Executive to take 
corrective action, to frame appropriate policies as well as to issue directives that will lead to 
improved financial management of organisations and contribute to better governance. 
This Chapter explains the planning and coverage of audit, response of departments and 
Government to audit findings/observations made during audit of transactions and follow-up 
action on previous Audit Reports.

of Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) under Audit jurisdiction of office of the 
Accountant General (Audit -I), Madhya Pradesh during the three -year period 2021 -24 is 
given below:

The Departments of GoMP has incorporated Information Technology (IT) applications 
extensively to enhance governance, transparency and service delivery. Through IT 
applications routine administrative processes have been automated reducing document 
mishandling, increasing efficiency, and promoting a paperless work environment. Total 147 
IT applications are being used by 1 7 out of 25 Departments of GoMP. The details of IT 
applications used by the Departments are given in Appendix 1.1.

1.3   Office of the Accountant General (Audit-I)  
Out of 55 departments of the 
Government of Madhya 
Pradesh, under the 
jurisdiction of Comptroller 
and Auditor General of India 
(CAG), Office of the 
Accountant General (Audit -
I), Madhya Pradesh, Gwalior 
is responsible for conducting 
audit of 25 Apex 
departments and Local 
Bodies/Public Sector 
Undertakings/Autonomous 
Bodies thereunder. Out of 
these, during the year 2023 -24, transaction audit of four Apex audit entity 2 was covered. 
Additionally, an expenditure of approximately ₹59,166.15 crore, representing 41.89 per cent 
of the total, was subjected to audits conducted for the year 2023-24. 
1.4 Authority for audit 
The CAG’s authority for audit is derived from Articles 149 to 151 of the Constitution of 
India, read with CAG's (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 (DPC Act). 
The CAG audits departments of the Government as per the following provisions of the DPC 
Act: 
• Audit of expenditure is carried out under Section 133 of the DPC Act; 
• Audit of PSUs is carried out under the Section 19(1)4 of the DPC Act; 
• Audit of autonomous bodies  is conducted under  Sections 19(2)5 and 20(1)6 of the DPC 
Act; 
• Local bodies are audited under Section 20(1) of the DPC Act; 
 
2     Principal secretary, Panchayat and Rural Development Department, Principal secretary, Home Department, 
Principal secretary, General Administration Department, Principal secretary, Revenue Department 
3 Audit of (i) all transactions from the Consolidated Fund of State (ii) all transactions relating to the 
Contingency Fund and Public Account and (iii) all trading, manufacturing, profit & loss accounts, balance 
sheets and other subsidiary accounts kept in any Department of a State. 
4 Audit of the accounts of Government companies is performed and exercised in accordance with the 
provisions of the Companies Act, 2013. 
5 Audit of accounts of Corporations (not being Companies) established by or under law made by State 
Legislature in accordance with provisions of the respective legislations. 
6 Audit of accounts of any body or authority on request of Governor, on such terms and conditions as may 
be agreed upon between CAG and Government. 
Office of the Accountant General 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
4 | P a g e  
• In addition, CAG also conducts audit of other autonomous bodies which are substantially 
funded by the Government under Section 147 of the DPC Act. 
Principles and methodologies for various audits are prescribed in Auditing Standards and 
the Regulations on Audit and Accounts, as well as other guidelines, manuals and instructions 
issued by or on behalf of the CAG. 
1.5 Planning and Conduct of audit 
The following flowchart depicts the process of planning and conduct of audit and 
preparation of Audit Reports: 
Figure-1.1: Planning and conduct of audit and preparation of Audit Reports
 
 
7 Audit of all (i) receipts and expenditure of a body/authority substantially financed by grants or loans from 
the Consolidated Fund of State and (ii) all receipts and expenditure of any body or authority where grants 
or loans to such body or authority from  the Consolidated Fund of State in a financial year is not less than 
₹one crore. 
Audit Report is prepared from  
• Important audit observations which featured in Inspection Reports or 
draft Performance Audit Reports/Compliance Audit Reports 
• The response of the Department/Government to audit findings are 
considered and 
• Submitted to Governor for causing it to be tabled in the State Legislature. 
 
Inspection Reports are issued based on  
• Scrutiny of records/data analysis 
• Examination of Audit evidence 
• Replies/Information furnished to Audit enquiries  
• Discussion with Head of the unit/local management 
Planning of Audit includes determining 
• Extent and type of Audit – Financial, Compliance and Performance audits 
• Audit objectives, scope and methodology of audit 
• Sample of auditee entities and transactions for detailed audit 
Assessment of Risk - Planning for audit of entities/schemes, etc., is based 
on risk assessment involving certain criteria like, 
• expenditure incurred  
• frequency of audit  
• criticality/complexity of activities 
• priority accorded for the activity by Government 
• level of delegated financial powers 
• assessment of internal controls  
• concerns of stakeholders, etc. 

Chapter-I: Overview 
5 | P a g e  
After completion of compliance audit of each unit, an Inspection Report (IR) containing 
audit findings is issued to the head of the unit with a request to furnish replies within one 
month of receipt of the IR. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled 
or further action for compliance is advised. Significant audit observations pointed out in 
these IRs, which require attention at the highest level in Government, are issued as draft 
paragraphs to the Government for their responses, befor e possible inclusion after due 
consideration of the responses, in the Audit Reports. In addition, draft Compliance Audits 
on specific themes, topics, schemes are also issued to the Government for their responses, 
before possible inclusion in the Audit Repo rts. These Audit Reports are submitted to the 
Governor of Madhya Pradesh under Article 151 of the Constitution of India for causing them 
to be laid on the Table of the State Legislature. 
1.6 Response of Departments to audit findings 
1.6.1 Response to previous Inspection Reports 
Heads of Offices and next higher authorities are required to respond to the observations 
contained in IRs and take appropriate corrective action. Audit observations communicated 
in IRs are also discussed at periodical intervals in Audit Committee meetings at 
District/State levels by officers of the Accountant General’s office with officers of the 
concerned Departments. 
As of March 2025, 13,771 IRs containing 56,244 paragraphs pertaining to previous years 
were pending for settlement as detailed below. Of these, first replies have not been received 
in respect of 12,211 paragraphs (21.71 per cent) contained in 1,420 IRs. Department -wise 
details are given in Appendix 1.2.

Lack of action on IRs and audit paragraphs is fraught with the risk of perpetuating serious 
financial irregularities pointed out in these reports. It may also result in dilution of internal 
controls in the governance process, inefficient and ineffective de livery of public goods/ 
services, fraud, corruption and loss to public exchequer. State Government, therefore, needs 
to institute an appropriate mechanism to review these IRs and audit paragraphs and take 
expeditious action to address the concerns flagged therein. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
6 | P a g e  
1.6.2 Response of Government to audit observations 
All departments are required to send their responses to draft performance audit 
reports/compliance audit reports/draft audit paragraphs proposed for inclusion in CAG’s 
Report within specified period8 of their receipt. We forwarded three compliance audit reports 
to the Additional Chief Secretary/Principal Secretaries of the  departments9 concerned, 
drawing their attention to the audit findings and requesting them to send their response 
within four  weeks. It was brought to their personal attention that these paragraphs were 
likely to be included in the Audit Report of the CAG of India, which would be placed before 
the State Legislature, and it would be desirable to include their comments/responses to the 
audit findings. Departments submitted the replies on compliance audits reports which have 
been appropriately incorporated in the Report.  
1.6.3 Response of Government to audit paragraphs that featured in earlier 
Audit Reports 
Administrative departments are required to submit Explanatory Notes (ENs) on paragraphs 
and reviews included in Audit Reports, within three  months10 of their presentation to State 
Legislature duly indicating action taken or proposed to be taken. For this purpose, the 
Departments are not required to wait for any notice or call from the Public Accounts 
Committee. Explanatory Notes were yet to be received from eight Departments in respect of 
227 paragraphs/performance audit review that featured in the Audit Reports for the year 
2020-21 and 2021-22 as of 31st March 2025. Details are given in Appendix-1.3.  
1.6.4 Response of Government to recommendations of the Public Accounts 
Committee 
Administrative departments are required to submit Action Taken Notes (ATNs) on 
recommendations of Public Accounts Committee (PAC) within six months 11 from the date 
of receipt of recommendations. As of March 2025, 22 ATNs on 47 paragraphs of Audit 
Reports in respect of eight Departments were yet to be received. Details are given in 
Appendix 1.4. 
1.7 Significant audit observations 
This Report contains findings of three compliance audits  that emerged from a test-check of 
accounts and transactions of three Departments12 of Government of Madhya Pradesh during 
2023-24.

Chapter-I: Overview 
7 | P a g e  
Significant results of audit that featured in this Report are summarised below. 
1.7.1   Audit on “Functioning of Mental Healthcare Establishments in the 
State” 
The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 ensures care and rights for persons with mental illness. 
The Madhya Pradesh Government set up the State Mental Health Authority (SMHA) in 2020 
to register and supervise Mental Health Establishments  (MHEs) and Professionals, set 
norms, handle complaints, and train staff. The National Mental Health Survey (2015 -16) 
found 13.90 per cent of the State’s population affected with mental illness, above the national 
average (10.70 per cent), with severe shortages of psychiatrists and infrastructure. The audit 
reviewed improvements in mental healthcare after SMHA’s formation. 
The Major audit finding are as follows: 
SMHA was constituted after a delay of two years and seven months (September 2020) and 
was not fully functional (July 2025). GoMP created ( January 2021 ) SMHA Fund for 
execution of activities of the SMHA and released ₹2.40 crore during 2020-24. However, no 
expenditure was incurred during 2020-22 and funds ranging from 46 to 89 per cent remained 
unutilised during 2022-24. Even after four years since its formation, only four of 15 members 
were nominated in the SMHA, no Mental Health Review Boards (MHRBs) or Mental Health 
Professoinals (MHPs) were registered, and no State -specific regulations were framed  for 
registration, renewal, and enforcement of minimum standards for Mental Health 
Establishments (MHEs) . Out of 163 MHEs, only 82 (50 per cent ) were provisionally 
registered; while the remaining 81 were operating without registration.  
 (Paragraph 2.1.5) 
Training and awareness activities mandated under the MH Act for school teachers, 
out-of-school children experiencing psycho -social problems and mental morbidity, as well 
as members of Gram Panchayats and block panchayats, were not implemented as planned 
training programmes were excluded from Annual Work Plans (2021 -24). Information, 
Education and Communication ( IEC) and awareness funds of ₹43.89 lakh remained 
unutilised as of June 2024 and shortfalls were observed in seminars and counselling sessions 
across test-checked districts. 
 (Paragraph 2.1.6) 
Essential medicines were not available in the test checked Government Mental Hospitals  
(GMHs), Government Medical Colleges (GMCs) , District Hospitals (DHs) and Community 
Health Center s (CHCs)  for periods ranging from one to 36 months during 2021 -24. 
Non-availability of required equipment as per norms of the Medical Council of India was 
also observed in the selected five GMCs during 2021 -24, with shortfall ranging from 47 to 
100 per cent.  
(Paragraphs 2.1.7.1 and 2.1.7.2) 
Basic amenities were found to be inadequate in the te st-checked GMHs, GMCs, and DHs, 
which lacked minimum essential facilities for mentally ill patients, including proper toilet 
sanitation, separate cots and pillows, designated wards and toilets for male and female 
patients, and access to safe drinking water. Additionally, none of the test-checked MHEs had 
separate accommodation for minors.  
(Paragraphs 2.1.7.5 and 2.1.7.9) 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
8 | P a g e  
Manpower shortages (as of March 2024) in test -checked MHEs ranged from 13 to 
67 per cent.  
 (Paragraphs 2.1.7.3) 
Due to non -establishment of Home-Based Rehabilitation Centre (HBRC)  for males in 
Gwalior and establishment of HBRC for females in Gwalior and Indore with a reduced bed 
capacity, 105 recovered patients were forced to stay alongside mentally ill patients, 
compromising their safety.  
(Paragraph 2.1.7.7) 
A total of 409 cases of Leave Against Medical Advice (LAMA) and absconding mental 
patients were reported in four MHEs. However, these MHEs lacked a system to track LAMA 
and absconding patients to ensure continuation of treatment. 
(Paragraph 2.1.7.11) 
Shortcomings were observed in the Tele Mental Health Assistance and Networking Across 
States programme, with 16 per cent of calls remaining unanswered during October 2022 -
March 2024. 
(Paragraphs 2.1.7.13) 
Against the required 14 meetings since its constitution (September 2020), SMHA held only 
one meeting (December 2020). SMHA did not conduct mandatory audits of the registered 
MHEs every three years to ensure compliance of minimum standards by the MHEs. It also 
did not prepare annual report for the period 2021 -24, which was required to be laid before 
the State Legislature. Further, the test checked DHs did not send annual calendar of planned 
activities to the State. These lapses resulted in weak oversight and ineffective monitoring of 
mental health services in the State. 
(Paragraph 2.1.8) 
Summary of recommendations 
GoMP may ensure:  
• registration of MHPs, classification of MHEs, and constitution of MHRBs by 
formulating State-specific regulations in line with the provisions of the MH Act.  
• timely circulation of AWP to districts for implementation of the planned activities and 
organization of awareness activities as planned.  
• Creation of a dashboard for real time monitoring of medicines and equipment 
availability.  
• filling up of vacancies and providing separate accommodation for minors in a time 
bound manner.  
• strengthening of follow -up mechanism for LAMA, absconding and discharged 
patients, and enhance Tele MANAS performance to provide more accessible care.  

Chapter-I: Overview 
9 | P a g e  
• conduct of SMHA meetings and supervision of MHEs as mandated, deployment of 
staffs in SMHA as per scale and preparation of annual reports/calendars to strengthen 
the monitoring mechanism.  
1.7.2 Audit on Functioning of Jiwaji University, Gwalior 
Jiwaji University (University), Gwalior was established in 1964 and is governed by Madhya 
Pradesh Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam 1973 (Act). The jurisdiction of the University extends 
across eight districts of the State. As of March 2024, 449 colleges (including 77 government 
colleges) are affiliated with the University. During the academic session 2023 -24, 3,661 
students in the de partments of the University and 1.16 lakh students were enrolled in the 
affiliated colleges. The National Assessment and Accreditation Council (NAAC) accredited 
(April 2023) the University with A++ grade.  
We conducted the audit of the University covering 2021-22 to 2023-24. The audit observed 
significant administrative, academic and financial shortcomings at the University.  
Good Practices 
The audit observed several good practices implemented by the University, which include: 
• Provision of a toll-free number to address and resolve students’ grievances efficiently. 
• Comprehensive information on the University’s website, covering Admission & Fee 
details, Academic programs, Research activities, Student Life, Alumni engagement, and 
Notice Board updates. 
• Organisation of a Diabetic Health Camp every Sunday at the Health Centre to promote 
early detection, effective management, and increased awareness of diabetes within the 
community. 
The major audit findings are given below: 
Statute 27  of the University provides that colleges shall be inspected by a Committee 
nominated by the Vice Chancellor. However, no such formal Committee was constituted by 
the University during 2021-24. 
(Paragraph 2.2.2.1(i)) 
Shortfalls were noticed in  meeting the minimum prescribed requirements for affiliation in 
31 test checked colleges , including land area, library books, laboratories with equipment, 
and facilities for sports, canteen, health care, toilets, parking, as well as safety and security 
measures.  
(Paragraph 2.2.2.1(ii)) 
The teachers and Principal were appointed by Management Committee of 23 affiliated 
colleges instead of Selection Committee as prescribed in the Statute. Further, delays were 
observed in granting affiliation to 29 colleges during 2021-24, which ranged between 49 and 
417 days.  
 (Paragraphs 2.2.2.1(iii) and (iv)) 
 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
10 | P a g e  
Seventeen of the 22 test-checked colleges did not adhere to the prescribed minimum lecture 
duration of one hour. 
 (Paragraph 2.2.2.2(ii)) 
The required approvals from the Bar Council of India (BCI) for running law courses in four 
colleges were not obtained before the commencement of sessions or the final admission 
dates during 2021-24.  Furthermore, mandatory BCI approval for 2023-24 was still pending 
(March 2025) in three colleges in Datia, Gwalior, and Shivpuri, where 238 students were 
admitted. 
 (Paragraph 2.2.2.3(i)) 
Distance education across two U ndergraduate and five P ostgraduate courses enrolled 
597 students during 2021-24. While examinations were conducted for first -year students 
admitted in 2021 -22 and 2022 -23, examinations for second -year undergraduate and 
final-year postgraduate students were not held. Further, as of December 2024, examinations 
for Undergraduate and Postgraduate courses of the 2023 -24 academic year was also not 
conducted. 
(Paragraph 2.2.2.5) 
Instead of recovering the entire cost (₹1.12 crore) for supply of low-quality answer books as 
per Clause 4 of the agreement, the University deducted only five per cent (₹ 4.99 lakh) of 
cost of supply, from the total bills of the two firms.  
(Paragraphs 2.2.2.6(i)) 
Delays in conduct of examinations ranged from four to 161 days and delay in declaration of 
results ranged from 19 to 380 days beyond the scheduled timelines during the academic 
years 2021 -22 to 2023 -24, adversely affected the academic calendar and negative ly 
impacted students’ future career prospects and higher education plans. The University had 
not implemented the facility for uploading degree certificates to DigiLocker. Out of 
2.43 lakh pass out students, degree were issued to 14,870 students during 2021-24.                              
 (Paragraphs 2.2.2.6(ii) and 2.2.2.6 (iii)) 
Significant teaching (72 and 42 per cent) and non-teaching posts (34 and 51 per cent) were 
vacant in University and test -checked colleges. Adequate infrastructure and essential 
facilities such as furniture, laboratory equipment, and security measures, etc. were not 
available in affiliated colleges and University to uphold academic standards.  
 (Paragraphs 2.2.3.1(ii to iv) and 2.2.3.2(ii)) 
Significant shortfalls in actual income (26 -44 per cent ) and substantial savings in 
expenditure (44-54 per cent) during 2021-24 indicate unrealistic budget estimates, which is 
further evidenced by the absence of income and expenditure demands from University 
departments during budget preparation.  
(Paragraph 2.2.4.1)  
The standard formats as required for preparing annual account of University were not 
prescribed by State Government. Income and Expenditure account of the University was 
prepared on cash basis. Investments of ₹306.31 crore were placed in 29 Fixed Deposit 

Chapter-I: Overview 
11 | P a g e  
Receipts (FDRs) as of March 2024. However, the University did not have a policy for 
investment of surplus funds, to ensure strategic financial decision-making.   
 (Paragraph 2.2.4.2) 
The GoMP sanctioned (November 2019) ₹16.49  crore for establishment of Centres of 
Excellence under eight components  in seven departments of University and a placement 
Cell, with activities to be completed by December 2020 . Against this, Higher Education 
Department released ₹ 8.35 crore (51 per cent), of which the university utilised ₹2.40 crore 
as of December 2024. The remaining amount of ₹5.95 crore  (71 per cent ) remained 
unutilised, primarily due to delays in the procurement process.  
(Paragraph 2.2.4.4 (i)) 
The University did not implement the Defined Contributory Pension Scheme (DCPS). 
During the period 2021 -24, it collected ₹11.43 crore towards employee and employer 
contributions from 429 employees but did not deposit the amount with the National 
Securities Depository Limited (NSDL) as prescribed. Instead, the contributions were 
retained in individual joint savings bank accounts (in the names of the employee and the 
Registrar), earning interest at rates ranging from 2.80 to 3.00 per cent, which resulted in a 
tentative loss of ₹1.08 crore to the 429 employees during the period 2021-24. 
 (Paragraph 2.2.4.5)  
The University operated 36 bank accounts linked to five cash books. However, no 
reconciliation was carried out and a difference of ₹7.79 crore was observed between the 
balances as per bank statements and the respective cash books as of March 2024. Further,  
fees received through MP Online, as well as fees collected through challans or demand 
drafts, were not reconciled with the actual receivable amounts based on University records.  
(Paragraph 2.2.4.6)  
The University had not developed mechanism for suo moto refund of caution money after 
completion of course. The refunds were made by the University on receiving applications 
from the students.  
(Paragraph 2.2.4.7) 
Internal audit wing was not established. In the absence of an internal audit mechanism, the 
University lacked a structured approach to monitor financial activities, thereby limiting its 
ability to prevent or detect financial irregularities . Further, Central Appraisal Committee 
was not formed in the University, indicating that the performance of self -financing courses 
was not reviewed and examined.  
(Paragraphs: 2.2.5.1 and 2.2.5.2) 
Summary of recommendations 
University may ensure  
• timely recruitment of teaching and non -teaching staff to reduce over -reliance on 
guest faculties and maintain operational continuity.  

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
12 | P a g e  
• adequate infrastructure and essential facilities are available in affiliated colleges 
and university, to uphold academic standards. 
• implementation of a financial management application using standardized forms for 
accrual-based annual accounts. 
• formulation and implementation of a policy for investment of surplus funds, to ensure 
strategic financial decision-making. 
• to put in place a mechanism to ensure timely transfer of contributions to Pension 
Fund Regulatory and Development Authority to safeguard the employee’s interest.  
• internal audit system is established to strengthen the internal control mechanism.  
1.7.3  Audit of Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission 
Government of India launched National Rurban Mission in February 2016 with objective to 
provide rural areas with economic development and enhanced enforcement of land reforms. 
The vision of the Mission was for development of a cluster of villages (Rurban Cluster) that 
preserve and nurture the essence of rural community life with focus on equity and 
inclusiveness alongwith the facilities perceived to be essentially urban in nature. 
Government of Madhya Pradesh selected 19 (11 tribal and eight non-tribal) growth clusters 
comprising of 167 Gram Panchayats for implementation over a five years period. 
We conducted the audit of the Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission covering the 
period of 2016-17 to 2023-24 in five (three tribal clusters and two non-tribal clusters) out of 
19 clusters.  
The Major audit finding are as follows: 
The Framework for Mission implementation, emphasized the preparation of a Spatial Plan 
as part of the Integrated Cluster Action Plan (ICAP) to ensure planned and coordinated 
development. However, the ICAP projects were executed without preparation of spatial plan 
for any cluster in the State.  
(Paragraph 2.3.2.2 (i)) 
The overall project implementation is significantly behind schedule with less than one-third 
of identified interventions completed. Out of 3,462 identified works in ICAPs, 2,078 works 
(60 per cent ) were approved. These included 1,074 (52 per cent ) completed, 
684 (33 per cent) incomplete and 320 (15 per cent) number of works not initiated. Critical 
sectors such as environment, LPG, tourism, sports infrastructure, and social welfare have 
seen zero progress.  
(Paragraph 2.3.3.1) 
The Mission guidelines required special emphasis on agriculture and allied activities while 
developing identified clusters. Out of 313 works approved under the category, 
150 (47.92 per cent) were completed, 86 (27.48 per cent) were in progress, and remaining 
were not initiated. It was observed that the implemented works largely focused on 
infrastructure-related activities such as the construction of check dams, stop dams, and 
recharge shafts. However, t he income -generating initiatives like Beekeeping and Honey 
Processing Plants, Dairy Development or Processing Units, Bulk Milk Chilling Machines, 

Chapter-I: Overview 
13 | P a g e  
Agri-Processing Units (for flour, pulses, vegetables, fruits, honey, etc.), Construction of 
Indigenous Seed Banks, Vegetable Cultivation Hubs, Collection Centres for 
Vegetables/Milk, and Community -Based Farming were either excluded from selection or 
selected but not initiated.  
(Paragraph 2.3.3.2 (i)) 
Out of 212 works identified for round the clock water supply in the 19 clusters in the State, 
40 works (18.87 per cent) were completed, 121 works (57.08 per cent) were in progress, 
and remaining were not initiated, indicating that the intended benefits of continuous water 
supply to households have not been fully realised.   
(Paragraph 2.3.3.2 (ii)) 
In 19 clusters, 171 social infrastructure construction works were initiated  under the social 
and basic infrastructure component , including bus stands, sports grounds, Anganwadi 
buildings, and more. However, 42 of these works remained incomplete, cancelled, 
suspended, or not started. 
(Paragraph 2.3.3.2. (iv)) 
The State Government did not issue any Service Level Benchmark s for assessment of 
various components in the Mission, as required under the Model Planning Guidelines for 
Rurban Clusters. This resulted in the absence of uniform standards to evaluate performance 
and measure outcomes, which in turn led to the non -assessment of multiple components of 
the Mission across different categories. 
 (Paragraph 2.3.3.4) 
Despite completion of 499 works worth ₹38.71 crore in selected Rurban clusters, Operation 
& Maintenance of these assets was not ensured even four years after Panchayat and Rural 
Development Department (PRDD) issued instructions. 
(Paragraph 2.3.3.5) 
Out of total expenditure incurred by the State, 86.86 per cent was met through convergence 
and only 13.14 per cent through the Critical Gap Fund (CGF), reflecting greater dependence 
on convergence resources and limited utilisation of CGF funds.  
(Paragraph 2.3.4.1) 
Against a project expenditure of ₹120.41 crore, Utilization Certificates (UCs) amounting to 
₹90.20 crore were submitted to the Government of India, resulting in a pendency of 
₹30.21 crore as of 30 September 2021. Similarly, for administrative expenditure of 
₹6.15 crore incurred during 2016‑17 to 2020‑21, UCs of ₹3.14 crore were submitted, leaving 
₹3.01 crore pending. Since the Books of Accounts for the years 2021‑22 to 2023‑24 were 
not made available for audit, the current status of expenditure and outstand ing UCs could 
not be ascertained.  
 (Paragraph 2.3.4.3) 
No records of field visits by the State Nodal Agency were available. While two ZPs (Bhopal 
and Dhar) confirmed no inspections, one ZP (Chhindwara) stated that State Level authorities 
had inspected, but, no records of the visit were made available.  

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
14 | P a g e  
As per PRDD order (August 2018), the District Level Committees (DLCs) were formed to 
watch the  progress of implementation of the Mission, release of funds, review of use of 
funds, field tours, establishment of convergence between various sectors and monitor the 
works. The DLCs were required to meet at least once in a quarter. However, a gainst the 
required 14 meetings during August 2018 to March 2024, a single meeting of the DLC was 
held in each selected cluster, resulting in inadequate monitoring of scheme activities. 
 (Paragraph 2.3.5.1 and 2.3.5.2) 
Joint physical verification of 140 works executed by GPs  showed that six works were 
incomplete, 84 works were executed without mandatory material testing, and 12 works were 
of substandard quality, indicating non-compliance with PRDD norms and associated risk of 
poor construction quality. 
(Paragraph 2.3.5.4) 
Summary of Recommendations  
The Government may ensure  
• completion of ongoing projects, which were committed under the Mission,  
• commencement of operation and maintenance activities of the revenue -generating 
projects by enabling proper ownership of the assets so created,  
• to fix responsibilities for deficient planning, implementation, monitoring and financial 
management. 
1.8 Acknowledgement 
The Office of the Accountant General (Audit -I), Madhya Pradesh, Gwalior wishes to 
acknowledge the co -operation and assistance rendered by the officials of the State 
Government during the course of conduct of audit.

Public Health and Medical Education Department 
2.1 Audit on “Functioning of Mental Healthcare Establishments in the 
State” 
2.1.1 Introduction 
Government of India (GoI) notified (April 2017) the Mental Healthcare Act (MH Act), 2017 
to provide mental healthcare and services for persons with mental illness and to protect, 
promote and fulfil the rights of such persons during delivery of mental healthcare.  
Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) es tablished (September 2020) the State Mental 
Health Authority (SMHA) under provisions of the MH Act. SMHA is responsible to register 
Mental Health Establishments13 (MHEs) and Mental Health Professionals (MHPs), develop 
quality and service provision norms for different types of MHEs  in the State, supervise all 
MHEs and receive complaints about deficiencies in healthcare services, and train all relevant 
persons.  
National Mental Health Survey (NMHS) 2015 -16 reported that 13.90 per cent (1.01 crore) 
of the population of the State was affected with mental illnesses which was higher than the 
national average ( nearly 10.70 per cent). The survey recommended for a standalone State 
Mental Health Policy by integrating mental health with the larger public health system, due 
to scarcity of infrastructure, poor implementation of existing policy and high treatment gap. 
The survey report also highlighted shortage of skilled human resources as the state had only 
0.05 psychiatrist per lakh population, which was far below the minimum requirement of one 
psychiatrist per lakh.  
SMHA was constituted in the state in the year 2020 and the audit was conducted to take an 
assurance on improvement in mental healthcare system post constitution of the SMHA.       
2.1.2 Organisational set-up 
Organisational set-up for delivery of mental healthcare services is given in Chart-2.1.1:  
 
  
 
13  Any health establishment, meant for the care of persons with mental illness, where persons with mental 
illness are admitted and reside at, or kept in, for care, treatment, convalescence and rehabilitation, either 
temporarily or otherwise.

Apart from above, the Social Justice and Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities 
(SJ&EPD) Department of the State Government is responsible for proper rehabilitation of 
recovered patients in the State. 
2.1.3 Audit objectives, Criteria, Scope and Methodology  
Audit was conducted to assess whether the State Government planned, designed and 
implemented activities in compliance with the provisions of the Act/Rules, Guidelines and 
GoI directions, sufficient infrastructure and facilities were available to provide quality health 
services to mental patients and a functional internal control system was in place. 
Audit criteria were sourced from the MH Act and Rules made thereunder; and Operational 
Guidelines, orders, circulars, list of essential drugs, facilitator's manual, survey, report 
handbooks issued by Central/State Government, National  Human Rights Commission of 
India (NHRCI) and Medical Council of India (MCI)/National Medical Commission 
(NMC)14. 
We conducted the audit by test checking of records of the Directorate of Medical Education 
(DME), Bhopal, National Health Mission, Bhopal and SMHA, Bhopal for the period 2021-
22 to 2023-24. Further, we selected five15 out of 13 districts having allopathic Government 
Medical Colleges  (GMC). In selected districts, we audited records of two Government 
Mental Hospitals16 (GMH), Psychiatry Department of five Government Medical Colleges, 
and records relating to National Mental Health Programme in five District Hospitals and 
District Health Societies (DHS) along with 10 Community Health Centres (two in each 
selected district ) to check availability of facilities for mental patients. Sampling 
methodology and details of selected samples are given in Appendix-2.1.1.  
An entry conference was held (November 2024) with Principal Secretary (PS), Public Health 
& Medical Education (PH&ME) Department to discuss the audit objectives, criteria, scope

Audit acknowledges the cooperation and assistance extended by the PH&ME Department 
and other functionaries for conducting the Compliance Audit. 
 
2.1.5 Compliance with the provisions of the Act/Rules 
2.1.5.1  Constitution of SMHA 
GoMP was required to constitute SMHA within nine months from the date (7 April 2017) 
on which the MH Act received the assent of the President. The SMHA would discharge 
functions and responsibilities specified under the MH Act for the promotion, regulation and 
monitoring of mental health services across the State.  
It is seen that GoMP constituted SMHA ( September 2020)  with a delay of two years and 
seven months17. Further, Finance Department, GoMP created ( January 2021) SMHA Fund 
for the execution of activities 18 of the SMHA and released ₹ 2.40 crore19 for the purpose 
during 2020-24. However, no expenditure was incurred from the fund during 2020 -21 and 
2021-22 and funds ranging 46 to 89 per cent  remained unutilized during 2022 -23 and 
2023-24.  
It was also observed that SMHA was not only constituted with delay but was also not fully 
operational (July 2025) to carry out all the activities mandated under the Act. Issues are 
discussed in succeeding 
paragraphs.  
As per data of National Crime 
Records Bureau (NC RB), 
suicide rate in the State was 
consistently increasing as 
shown in Chart 2.1.2 . The

reports state that  suicides may 
result from multiple causes 
such as professional or career 
problems, isolation, abuse, 
violence, family issues, mental 
disorders, addiction to alcohol, financial loss, or chronic illness. While some of these factors

effectively through MHEs. A fully functional SMHA, as the regulatory and monitoring body, 
could positively impact suicide reduction. 
Government in its reply attributed (July 2025) reasons for delay in establishment of SMHA 
to lack of fund, infrastructure and mental health professionals in the State and assured that 
SMHA fund would be utilized as per the requirement.  
2.1.5.2  Nomination of members of SMHA 
Section 46 (1) of the MH Act states that the SMHA shall comprise of chairperson ex officio20 
and 15 members (details are given in Appendix-2.1.2) from departments of health, medical 
education and social welfare/relevant ministries of the State Government/mental hospital. 
GoMP through notification ( September 2020 ) nominated Principal Secretary, PH&ME 
Department as Chairperson and four members of the SMHA.  
We noticed that GoMP did not appoint 11 members (as of May 2024) as detailed in 
Appendix-2.1.2 in the SMHA as per requirement of the MH Act even after lapse of four 
years since its formation ( September 2020). Thus, SMHA deprived of inputs of eminent 
experts towards its objectives as envisaged in the MH Act. 
Government accepted the observations (July 2025) and assured that remaining members of 
the SMHA would be nominated soon.  
2.1.5.3  Formation of Mental Health Review Boards (MHRBs) 
The MH Act stipulates  that the SMHA shall, by notification, constitute MHRBs, ensuring 
oversight and protection of the rights of persons with mental illness. Further, as per the 
Mental Healthcare (CMHA and MHRBs) Rules 2018, at least one MHRB was to be 
constituted for a district and where it is not feasible one MHRB shall be constituted for a 
group of two or more districts, not exceeding three districts in the State. The main function 
of MHRB is to protect the rights of persons with mental illness and ensure that they receive 
proper care and treatment. 
It was seen that SMHA did not form MHRBs in any district for redressal of concerns of 
mental patients even after lapse of four years since its constitution. In absence of MHRBs, 
aggrieved persons were deprived of right to redressal of their concerns, leading to violation 
of rights and ultimately defeating the Act’s purpose of providing humane and rights -based 
mental healthcare. 
Government accepted the observations (July 2025) and stated that action for formation of 
MHRBs is under process.   
2.1.5.4  Registration of Mental Health Professionals (MHPs) 
The MH Act stipulates  that the SMHA shall register clinical psychologists, mental health 
nurses and psychiatric social workers in the State to work as MHPs. 
 
20  Secretary or Principal Secretary in the Department of Health of State Government  

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
20 | P a g e  
It was seen that SMHA did not register MHPs (as of July 2025). In absence of registration, 
the SMHA did not have data of MHPs in the State to effectively plan healthcare facilities 
for mental patients. 
Government accepted the observations (July 2025) and stated that process for registration 
of MHPs has now been initiated.  
Reply is not acceptable as registration of MHPs should have been done on priority since it 
was essential for mapping and associating MHPs with the services rendered.  
2.1.5.5  Classification of MHEs 
As per Section 65 (4) of the MH Act,  the SMHA was required to classify mental health 
establishments into different categories, specify different standards for different categories 
of MHEs and  minimum standards for MHEs,  have regard to local conditions through a 
notification within a period of eighteen months from the commencement of the Act. Further, 
the SMHA was to make regulations, consistent with the provision of the MH Act and the 
rules made thereunder, to carry out the provisions of the MH Act.  
NMHS Survey (2015 -16) also recommended for development of a State specific mental 
health policy, focusing on the treatment, rehabilitation and creating awareness as per 
prevalence and dynamics of the mental morbidities. 
It was observed that the SMHA had not formulated regulations for the registration, renewal, 
and enforcement of minimum standards for Mental Health Establishments (MHEs). It also 
did not classify MHEs into different categories, even after lapse of four years ten months 21 
since its establishment. Further, it was seen that SMHA adopted the Central Mental Health 
Authority (CMHA) Regulations 2020 of GoI  instead of framing its own State specific 
regulations considering the local conditions in the State.  
Non-framing of regulations considering the local conditions and minimum standards for 
MHEs indicates lack of adequate action of the SMHA in ensuring quality and right based 
mental health care for the mental patients. 
Government accepted the observations (July 2025) and assured that regulations for 
classifying and registering MHEs, as provided in the MH Act, would be framed soon.  
2.1.5.6  Registration of MHEs 
The MH Act stipulates that no person or organisation shall establish or run an MHE unless 
it has been registered with the SMHA under the provisions of the MH Act. It further provides 
for imposition of penalty of ₹ 5,000 on MHEs found operational without  registration. 
Further, The Mental Healthcare (SMHA) Rules 2018  provides for collection of  a fee of 
₹ 20,000 for provisional registration.  
It was seen that out of 163 22 MHEs available in the State (July 2024), only 82 MHEs  
(50 per cent) were provisionally registered by SMHA and remaining 81 MHEs were running 
without registration as detailed in  Appendix-2.1.3. SMHA neither took action to register 
 
21  Since constitution of SMHA (September 2020) upto exit conference (July 2025). 
22  As per SMHA, PH&ME and SJ&EPD Department and information available on concerned websites.  

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
21 | P a g e  
these MHEs nor imposed penalty due to which quality healthcare facility in the MHEs could 
not be ensured apart from loss of registration fee of ₹ 16.20 lakh. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that due to lack of staff, 
registration of MHEs and imposition of penalty could not be done.  
2.1.5.7  Delay in approval of registration 
The Section 66 (4) of the MH Act stipulates  that the SMHA, within a period of ten days 
from the date of receipt of application, issue to the MHE a certificate of provisional 
registration.  
Further, Section 66 (14) ibid stipulates that as soon as the MHE submits the required 
evidence of the mental health establishment having complied with the specified minimum 
standards, the Authority shall give public notice and display the same on its website for a 
period of thirty day s, for filing objections, if any, in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
It was observed that the SMHA issued the approval of provisional registration of 38 MHEs 
(out of 82 MHEs) with delays between two and 34 days as shown in Appendix-2.1.4. 
Further, there was delay in publication of information of 37 MHEs (out of 82 MHEs) 
between two and 115 days as shown in Appendix-2.1.5. 
Government accepted the observations and ensured (July 2025) that no further delay would 
occur, as the registration process has now been made online. 
2.1.6  Planning and implementation of awareness activities 
2.1.6.1  Inclusion of prescribed training programmes in the Annual Work Plan

public health programmes to 
reduce suicides and attempted 
suicides in the country. Further, 
GoI issued ( June 2015 ) 
guidelines for implementation of

suggested to focus on creating 
awareness among school going 
children, adult member of the community and community leaders. 
Despite the findings of the NMHS Report 2015-16, which highlighted the alarming suicide 
rates comparing to the national average (as of 2014), as depicted in Chart 2.1.3, the Mission 
Director, NHM, Bhopal did not incorporate training programmes for school teachers, 
out-of-school children experiencing psycho -social problems and mental morbidity, as well

2.1.6.2  Delay in circulation of Annual Work Plan to districts for implementation 
The Operational Guidelines of NHM stipulates that  the beginning of the financial year i.e. 
1st of April is the enforcement date of the Project Implementation Plan (PIP) and districts 
were required to submit annual PIPs to State for getting approval up to 15th March. 
It was seen that NHM, Bhopal prepared PIPs at their own level without obtaining PIPs from

2.1.6.3   Implementation of activities to disseminate awareness  
National Mental Health Policy of India (2014) stipulates  that reliable mental health 
information should be readily available to protect and promote mental health and well-being 
of people. Mass media events and outreach programmes should be organised to disseminate 
such information. To supplement information cou nselling services, helplines, dedicated 
websites should be made available for easy flow of information. 
It was seen that Mission Director, NHM was allocated ₹ 20 lakh  for printing of Information, 
Education and Communication (IEC) materials23, during the years 2022-2324 and 2023-2425, 
which remained unutilized at the end of the year. Similarly, NHM had a separate fund of 
₹ 23.89 lakh for observation of days (such as World Mental Health Day) and other related 
IEC activities in 2022-23, which was also not utilized (as of June 2024). Inability to utilize 
above funds indicates non -implementation of these activities to disseminate public 
awareness as intended under the program objectives. 
Government accepted the observations and ensured (July 2025) that efforts would be made 
to fully utilize the fund on awareness activities.  
2.1.6.4  Shortfall in organising the mental health awareness seminars 
NHM, Madhya Pradesh, instructed26 all Civil Surgeons (CS) and Chief Medical and Health 
Officers (CMHOs) across districts to organize mental health awareness seminars in 
accordance with the approved AWP. Under this plan, budgetary allocations were made to the 
respective officers to conduct a series of seminars at both district and block levels, targeting 
personnel and the individuals who serve, in schools, colleges, jails, police department, old 
age homes, etc. to enhance awareness of mental health and stress management.

It was seen that test checked districts either did not organize mental health awareness

Further, DH Indore conducted only two seminars in schools, falling short of the three

seminars, as against the three planned for the same period. Similarly, DH Khandwa  
conducted two seminars in colleges, compared to the three planned in the AWP 2022–24. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025)  that these are routine and 
relatively small activities but were crucial for promoting mental health and preventing 
mental illness and must be carried out diligently.  
The reply lacks commitment in ensuring targeted seminars in future. 
2.1.6.5  Lack of counselling and awareness sessions for school students 
As per AWP 2023 -24, Counselling Cell was to be formed in two eminent government 
schools of the district where clinical psychologist would examine the mental health of 
students, discuss about the mental health of students with their parents and held a awareness 
workshop. Twenty counselling and awareness sessions were to be organized per district in a 
year. CS/CMHOs of the districts were responsible for implementation of activities of AWP. 
It was seen in test checked DHs ( except Khandwa)27 that against 20 counselling sessions, 
there was shortfall of nine and two counselling and awareness sessions for school students 
in  Gwalior and Indore districts respectively. Further, no psychological test of the students 
was performed in both Gwalior and Indore districts. It was further seen that meetings with 
parents for mental health of their children and awareness workshops and discussions for 
teachers were not organized in any of the selected districts. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that, while routine and 
relatively minor, these activities are essential for promoting mental health and preventing 
mental illness, and must be carried out diligently.  
2.1.7  Availability of healthcare facilities 
 
2.1.7.1  Availability of essential medicines 
As per National List of Essential Medicines (NLEM) 2011 of GoI, Government Mental 
Hospitals (GMHs) and Government Medical Colleges (GMCs)  were required to maintain 
stock of  10 (14 since September 2022) essential drugs for the treatment of mentally ill 
patients. Further, the Directorate of Health Services instructed in February 2021, October 
2021 and July 2022 to ensure availability of 10, 20 and 23 essential medicines respectively 
in District Hospitals (DHs) and five, 15 and three essential medicines respectively in CHCs. 
Furthermore, as per MP Drug Policy, 2009, minimum three months stock for district level 
hospitals and two months for below level was to be maintained.  The MH Act also provides 
that the appropriate Government shall ensure the availability of free of cost medicines to all 
 
27  Activity was not included in AWP 2023-24 of DH Khandwa 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
25 | P a g e  
persons with mental illness at all times at health establishments run or funded by 
Government starting from CHCs and upwards in the public health system. 
It was seen that essential medicines were not available in test checked GMHs (one to five 
medicines), GMCs (one to 13 medicines), DHs (one to nine medicines) and CHCs (one to 
15 medicines) for one to 36 months as detailed in Appendix-2.1.8. Medicines are a core 
component of treatment, and it is assessed that its absence affected the quality of treatment 
to patients with mental illness. 
Government stated (July 2025) that essential medicines listed in the NLEM was not to be 
adhered to mandatorily in tertiary care hospitals i.e. GMHs and GMCs as health was a state 
subject and having its own EML and ensured that the matter would be examined, particularly 
with regard to DHs and CHCs.   
The reply was not acceptable as NLEM provided list of essential medicines to be kept in 
tertiary care hospitals. Further, we did not find GoMP order regarding essential medicines 
to be maintained in tertiary care hospitals. In absence of specific order for  essential 
medicines to be kept in tertiary care hospitals, these hospitals were required to maintain 
essential medicines as provided in NLEM. Non-availability of essential medicines may lead 
to risk of patients being deprived of timely access to essential medicines.

for medical colleges based on annual admission28 of MBBS students.

MCI norms during 2021 -24 as detailed in Appendix-2.1.9 and summary shown in

at DHs and CHCs is vital to cater the mental healthcare need of the population. Due

interventions in mental health. The onus of providing mental health services is upon 
the DH, where caseloads for OPD are high.

examined and intimated to audit in due course. In response to creation of posts under NMHP, 
it was stated that three posts were sanctioned based on need assessment.  
However, copy of need assessment was not provided along with the reply for verification by 
the audit. It may be concluded that the GoMP did not sanction adequate mental healthcare 
staff for treatment of patients with mental illness in the state.

2.1.7.4  Deployment of Radiologist in Mental Hospitals 
As per Minimum Standards of Care in GMHs issued (2000) by National Institute of Mental 
Health and Neurosciences, all GMHs should have facilities for X -Ray, ECG and facilities 
for psychodiagnostics assessments etc. 
It was seen that no Radiologist (against the sanction of one in each GMH) was posted in any 
of the two GMHs during 2021-24. We noticed that 417 X-rays38 in GMH Gwalior and 168939 
X-rays, ECGs and EEGs in GMH Indore were done. In absence of Radiologist, the images  
were being analysed by the existing medical team. The Psychiatrists provided treatment to 
the patients directly without getting analysis of the images by Radiologist. 
Government stated (July 2025) that action for filling up the vacant posts was being initiated. 
2.1.7.5  Lack of facilities in MHEs 
The Mental Health Care (CMHA) Regulations, 2020 provides  minimum standards viz. 
pucca structure with functional windows and doors, separate cots, mattresses, pillows and 
blankets, hygiene, sanitation, separate wards for male and female inpatients, surrounding 
space between the beds etc. for MHEs. Further, National Human Rights Commission 
(NHRC) publication (December 2012)  envisages that the mentally ill persons should be 
ensured a life of dignity and there is a need to provide them with good quality care and 
treatment.  
It was seen during Joint Physical Verification (JPV) that test checked GMHs, GMCs and 
DHs lacked minimum facilities for mentally ill patients viz. sanitation in toilets, separate 
cots and pillows for patients, regular cleaning and changing the linen, separ ate wards and 
toilets for male and female patients, drinking water facilities as detailed in Appendix-2.1.12 
and illustrated in Photographs-2.1.1 to 2.1.4 showing non -availability of minimum 
standards in MHEs.

2.1.7.6  Utilization of funds for establishment of CoE  
GoI provided fund to establish CoE by upgrading and strengthening identified existing 
mental hospitals for addressing acute manpower gap and provisions of state of the art 
facilities for  mental health care in the long run. The support would involve capital wo rk40, 
equipment and furnishing, faculty induction and retention. MH Gwalior and Indore were 
selected under CoE scheme. The fund sharing ratio between Central and State Government 
was 60 per cent and 40 per cent respectively. 
2.1.7.6 (a) Irregularity in release and utilization of fund for upgradation 
It was observed that GoI released (February and March 2016) ₹ 4.76 crore 41 to DME for 
establishment of CoE at the GMH Gwalior. However, DME did not release (as of June 2024) 
the amount along with State's matching share of ₹ 3.18 crore to GMH Gwalior due to delay 
in administrative approval, resulting in non -development of facilities for treatment of 
mentally ill patients apart from idling of GoI fund for eight years. 
 
40  Academic block, library, hostel, lab, supportive departments, lecture theaters etc.

It was further observed that on the basis of copy of sanction letter issued (February 2016) 
by GoI for release of funds, NHM Bhopal erroneously released (August 2016) the amount 
of ₹ 3.25 crore to GMH Gwalior from its own funds. NHM made three corresponden ces 
with DME for refund, but the amount was not refunded. 
Further, GMH Gwalior incurred an expenditure of ₹ 91.60 lakh on maintenance and 
housekeeping services  which was against the prescribed components 42 under CoE  and 
₹ 9.19 lakh through Madhya Pradesh Laghu Udyog Nigam (MPLUN)  for which it did not 
have details of expenditure. Rest of amount was spent on prescribed components of CoE.  
Accordingly, GMH, Gwalior sent (August 2019) Utilization Certificate of ₹ 3.25 crore to 
GoI. Thus, MH Gwalior incurred expenditure of ₹ 1.01 crore on purposes which were not 
related to components of the CoE. 
Government accepted the observations and assured (July 2025) to take corrective action.  
2.1.7.6 (b)  Delay in release of funds 
It was observed that GoI sanctioned (December 2016) and released (January 2017) 
₹ six crore for upgradation of GMH Indore under CoE with directions to ensure release of 
fund with State's matching share at the earliest.  
DME, Bhopal released (between June 2023 and April 2024) the GoI grant along with State's 
matching share of ₹ four crore to the implementing agencies43 with delays ranging from six 
years and four months44 to seven years and two months45. Significant delay in fund affected 
the upgradation work and the beneficiaries remained deprived of better facilities. 
Commissioner, DME stated (January 2025) that delay was occurred due to delay in receipt 
of administrative approval for the proposal.  
The reply is not acceptable as the stated reason did not justify the delay of seven years.  
2.1.7.7  Establishment of Home-Based Rehabilitation Centre (HBRC) 
The MH Act provides for home-based rehabilitation46 for mentally ill patients. Further, in 
compliance of instructions of Hon’ble Supreme Court , the Social Justice and 
Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities (SJ&EPD) Department, GoMP directed 
(July 2017) to establish two HBRC in district Gwalior and one in district Indore, each with 
a capacity of 50 seats. Separate arrangements in these HBRCs were to be made for males 
and females.

2.1.7.8  Absence of quality food to mental patients 
NHRC Report, 2012 envisages that mentally ill patients have right to wholesome, balanced 
and nutritional food according to prescribed norms i.e. 2,500 and 3,000 kilo calories for 
women and men respectively. Medical Education Department, GoMP prescribed (June 
2019) ₹ 48 per IPD patient per day for expenditure on food.  
It was seen that NHRC recommended to increase amount for food to ₹ 80 for GMH Indore 
(November 2022) and ₹ 75 for GMH Gwalior (January 2024) by stating that the amount was 
not adequate to provide prescribed food items to IPD patients. We noticed that GMH 
Gwalior and Indore spent (during 2021 -24) ₹ 65.4347 and ₹ 48 per patient  per day 
respectively on food of IPD patients. The Commissioner, DME did not increase the 
prescribed rates despite repeated requests ( July 2021, May 2022 and September 2022) of 
GMH Indore. We further noticed that no post of dietician was sanctioned in GMH Gwalior 
and it was sanctioned but was vacant in GMH Indore . Thus, the department did not take 
action to increase the amount as recommended by the NHRC and posting of dietician in 
GMHs resulting in patients being deprived of the quality food.  
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that process for making 
minimum standards and regulations was under process. 
2.1.7.9  Separate accommodation for minors 
The MH Act stipulates that a minor admitted in an MHE shall be accommodated separately 
from adults, in an environment that takes into account his age and developmental needs. 
It was seen that during 2021 -24, no separate accommodation was available for minors in 
any of the test checked MHEs. As a result, 306 minors48 were forced to stay with adults in 
male and female wards in selected MHEs.

2.1.7.10  Follow-up of discharged patients 
The MH Act stipulates that whenever a person undergoing treatment for mental illness in an 
MHE is to be discharged into the community, the psychiatrist responsible for the person’s 
care shall develop a follow-up plan for the mentally ill person. 
It was seen that 5,769 patients 49 were discharged from selected GMHs, GMCs and DHs 
during 2021-24, without any follow-up program. Thus, psychiatrist, responsible for care of 
mental patient, did not provide  further direction to the patient through follow -up plan. In 
absence of treatment plan, the possibility of recurrence of mental illness cannot be ruled out. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) follow -up of discharged 
patients would be ensured in future.  
2.1.7.11  Tracking of Leave Against Medical Advice/absconding patients  
Leave Against Medical Advice ( LAMA) and absconding of patients are performance 
indicator showing quality of healthcare service and negligence in security services 
respectively.  
It was seen that there were 409 cases 50 of LAMA and absconding mental patients in four 
MHEs. These MHEs did not have system to track LAMA and absconding patients to ensure 
that these patients had taken treatment. During JPV ( January 2025), it was found that no 
security staff was available in Psychiatric Department of GMC, Gwalior . This indicates 
unsatisfactory medical healthcare facilities and lack of security services in MHEs.  
In response to the observation, the MHEs acknowledged the issue and attributed instances 
of patient absconding to inadequate vigilance and insufficient training of security personnel 
in handling patients. They further assured that preventive measures such as increasing 
security staff, installing CCTV cameras, restricting entry/exit points, conducting regular 
staff training, and enhanced monitoring of high -risk patients would be undertaken. SMHA 
stated (June 2025) that preparation of minimum standards was under finalization. 
2.1.7.12  Adherence of fire safety norms 
Public Health and Family Welfare Department, GoMP issued (November 2021) instructions 
to all Divisional Commissioners/Collectors and government medical institutions, to take 
action to prevent fire accidents in all medical colleges, district hospitals and civil hospitals 
as per protocol of basic fire and electrical safety and obtain fire NOC for health institutions, 
organising training and rehearsal two times in a year and ensure regular fire and electrical 
audit. Copy of the instructions was also sent to head of all government medical institutions. 
 
49  GMH Gwalior -251, MH Indore -2655, GMC Bhopal -1116, GMC Gwalior -211, GMC Ratlam -97, DH 
Bhopal-55, DH Khandwa-1289 and DH Ratlam- 95

GMC Bhopal: Abs- 113 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
34 | P a g e  
It was seen that G MH Gwalior, GMC Ratlam, DH Bhopal, Gwalior and Khandwa did not 
have fire NOC during 2022-24. Training and exercises on fire safety (twice a year) were not 
conducted in any test checked healthcare institutions except GMC Bhopal  (once in June 
2023 during 2022-24 against required four). Fire and electrical audits were not conducted in 
GMC Gwalior and DH Gwalior. Old electrical wires and loose wires were not replaced in 
DH Bhopal, Gwalior, Khandwa and Ratlam. 
Thus, head of above MHEs did not take action to ensure compliance with the fire safety 
norms. Non-obtaining the fire NOC compromises the safety of patients and staff, exposing 
the facility to significant fire hazards and regulatory non-compliance. 
SMHA stated (June 2025) that preparation of minimum standards was under finalization.  
The reply is not acceptable as non -adherence of fire safety norms may lead to loss of life, 
injury and destruction of critical infrastructure in case of fire.  
2.1.7.13 Implementation of Tele MANAS 
2.1.7.13 (a)  Deficiencies in Call Connectivity and Follow-up Mechanism  
As per GoI's Operational Guidelines for National Tele Mental Health Programme (NTMHP), 
Tele Mental Health Assistance and Networking Across States (Tele MANAS) envisions to 
work as a comprehensive, integrated and inclusive 24x7 Tele Mental Health Facility. As per 
the Guidelines, counsellor would be a trained non -specialist delivering mental healthcare 
through Tele MANAS. Approximately 15 to 30 minutes was to be spent by each counsellor 
on each call depending on the case. The indicators for monitoring of Tele MANAS are 
number of calls attended in Tier-1 and Tier-2, response time, satisfaction surveys with users 
and patient level outcomes. 
It was seen from analysis of data of Tele MANAS Cell for the period October 2022 to 
March 2024 that 13,869 (16 per cent ) out of 85,260 calls 51 were not connected in Tele 
MANAS Cell Gwalior and Indore. It was further seen that 16,238 (81 per cent) calls in 
GMH, Gwalior and 32,055 (77 per cent) calls52 in GMH, Indore were disconnected in less 
than the prescribed minimum time of 15 minutes as detailed in Appendix 2.1.13. 
Further, State Tele MANAS cells referred patients for in-person consultation at Tier-II level 
in various districts. However, neither the State Tele MANAS Cells nor the DHs had data of 
referred patients taken consultation as detailed in Table-2.1.3. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
51   2909 out of 26173 calls in Gwalior and 10960 out of 59087 calls in Indore. Reasons shown were – attempt 
failed, busy, call_hangup, call_not_picked, failed, provider_temp_failure. 
52  Overall, 78 per cent of calls were disconnected before the prescribed minimum duration of 15 minutes, of 
which 66 per cent ended within the first five minutes

The test -checked MHEs also confirmed  that no person was appeared for in -person 
consultation which indicates absence of follow-up of referred patients. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that duration of calls largely 
depends on the willingness of the patients/caregivers. It was further stated that a missed call 
system was implemented in November 2024, and system would be developed to track the 
patients referred through Tele MANAS to ensure in-person consultation.  
Reply is not acceptable since 66 per cent of calls were disconnected within five minutes 
which indicate inadequate delivery of counselling service to the patients which is the core 
objective of the programme. Furthermore, follow-up of referred patients was a key outcome 
indicator, which appears to have been insufficiently addressed.  
2.1.7.13 (b)  Irregular procurement of equipment for Tele MANAS  
GoI provided (August 2022) funds to NHM, Bhopal for procurement of IT equipment i.e. 
Laptop, IP phone etc. for implementation of Tele MANAS programme. 
It was seen that Mission Director, NHM Bhopal sanctioned (September 2022) and procured 
(between October 2022 and March 2023) 20 Internet Protocol (IP) phones  costing 
₹ 1.99 lakh, for Tele MANAS Cells and 102 Laptops costing ₹ 48.66 lakh, for establishing 
referral linkages at DHs. The IP phones were found lying idle in Tele MANAS Cells and the 
Senior Consultant of Mentoring Institute, AIIMS, Bhopal remarked (March 2023) that there 
was no use of IP Phones in Tele MANAS Cells since audio/video consultation services were 
not yet started in OPD of DHs (Mankaksha55). Further, it was informed that the laptops, were 
used for making daily entries of OPD data in SMART Portal instead of intended purpose of 
filling data of cases referred from Tele -MANAS to Man -Kaksha.Thus, equipment costing 
₹ 50.65 lakh remained idle or utilized for other purposes for more than one year. 
Government accepted (July 2025) the observation and stated that IP phones were procured 
in accordance with GoI guidelines. The Mankaksha (DMHP) staff is using laptops for entry 
of daily OPD data in SMART Portal, preparing monthly report, for online review meetings 
etc. also.  
The reply is not acceptable as IP Phones were procured without assessing the need and the 
use of laptops for purposes other than referral linkages indicates non -compliance with the 
intended objectives of the scheme.

2.1.8  Monitoring Mechanism 
2.1.8.1  Shortfall in SMHA meetings 
The MH Act provides that the SMHA shall meet at such times but not less than four times 
in a year. 
It was seen that since constitution (September 2020) of the SMHA, SMHA meeting was held 
only once (December 2020) against the required 14 meetings 56. Non-conduct of periodic 
meetings may lead to mismanagement, delayed decisions, and potential shortcomings in 
activities performed by SMHA. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that SMHA meetings would 
be held as required in the MH Act.  
2.1.8.2  Short deployment of staff in SMHA 
Chief Executive Officer, SMHA was responsible for execution of day to day functioning of 
the SMHA. Further, NHM approved (March 2020) posts for the functioning of the SMHA.  
It was seen that against 10 sanctioned posts, the Department deployed only three regular 
staff in SMHA, four posts were filled either by attachment or through additional charge and 
three posts remained vacant (as detailed in Appendix 2.1.14). Shortage of staff adversely 
affected the implementation of the MH Act in the State, as pointed out in paragraphs 2.1.5.6 
and 2.1.5.7. 
In its reply (June 2025), SMHA stated that NHM staff have been attached against seven out 
of ten vacant posts.  
The reply is not acceptable, as the absence of regularly appointed staff for SMHA would 
adversely affect its effective functioning. 
2.1.8.3  Supervision of MHEs 
The MH Act provides that the SMHA is required to audit all registered MHEs every three 
years to ensure compliance of minimum standards by the MHEs.  
It was seen that SMHA did not arrange for audit of MHEs to ascertain availability of 
minimum standards in MHEs, in absence of which, the availability of minimum facilities in 
MHEs could not be ensured. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that the supervision of MHEs 
is under process.  
2.1.8.4  Preparation of annual report and annual calendar 
The MH Act stipulates that the SMHA was required to prepare every year an annual report 
giving a full account of its activities during the previous year which was to be laid before 
the State Legislature. Further, as per the work plan issued (June 2021, August 2022 and 
June 2023) by NHM for execution of activities under NMHP. Medical Officer in charge of 
 
56  During September 2020 to March 2024. 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
37 | P a g e  
Mankaksh of the concerned district has to prepare activity wise annual calendar of 
proceedings and send it to the State. 
It was seen that SMHA did not prepare annual report for the period 2021 -24. Further, test 
checked DHs did not send annual calendar comprising activities planned for the year. Timely 
preparation of annual report and calendar was necessary for monitoring of the activities of 
the SMHA. 
Government accepted the observations and stated (July 2025) that preparation of annual 
calendar is under process.  
2.1.9  Conclusion  
GoMP constituted SMHA to discharge the functions and responsibilities specified under the 
MH Act with a delay of two years and seven months. Even after its constitution, SMHA 
functioned to a limited extent due to non -nomination of eminent experts and shortage of 
staff, resulting in non-compliance with provisions of the MH Act, including non-constitution 
of MHRBs, non-registration of MHPs, absence of prescribed minimum standards for MHEs, 
non-classification of MHEs, absence of State -specific regulations, and  non-registration or 
delays in registration of MHEs. 
The Department did not include the prescribed training programmes in the AWP, delayed 
the circulation of the AWPs, did not implement activities to disseminate awareness about 
mental illness, and fell short in conducting mental health awareness seminars and 
counselling sessions. MHEs lacked essential medicines for up to 36 months, had equipment 
shortfalls ranging from 47 per cent to 100 per cent, and suffered human resource shortages 
between 13 per cent and 67 per cent, affecting the quality of treatment. The MHEs lacked 
minimum basic facilities and the hospital buildings were found in dilapidated condition 
having dampness on walls and dripping from the roof and non-usable toilets and bathrooms. 
Further, none of the test-checked MHEs had separate accommodation for minors. Delay of 
seven years in fund release by DME and diversion of ₹1.01 crore of CoE funds to non -
admissible purposes further impact ed service delivery. Home -based rehabilitation centres 
were not adequately established, and there was no follow -up mechanism for discharged, 
LAMA, or absconding patients, increasing risks of relapse. The implementation of Tele 
MANAS was also deficient as 16 per cent  calls went unanswered and no follow -up 
mechanism was in place . Weak monitoring was evident from infrequent SMHA meetings, 
absence of MHE supervision, inadequate staffing, and non-preparation of annual reports and 
calendars.  
The Audit is of the opinion that Mental Health Establishments in the State were functioning 
with significant deficiencies and did not conform to the prescribed standards.  
 
 
 
 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
38 | P a g e  
2.1.10  Recommendations 
GoMP may ensure:  
• registration of MHPs, classification of MHEs, and constitution of MHRBs by 
formulating State-specific regulations in line with the provisions of the MH Act. (with 
reference to paragraphs 2.1.5.1 to 2.1.5.7) 
• timely circulation of AWP to districts for implementation of the planned activities and 
organization of awareness activities as planned. (with reference to paragraphs 2.1.6.1 
to 2.1.6.5)  
• Creation of a dashboard for real time monitoring of medicines and equipment 
availability. (with reference to paragraphs 2.1.7.1 and 2.1.7.2) 
• filling up of vacancies and providing separate accommodation for minors in a time 
bound manner. (with reference to paragraphs 2.1.7.3 and 2.1.7.9) 
• strengthening of follow-up mechanism for LAMA, absconding and discharged patients, 
and enhance Tele MANAS performance to provide more accessible care. (with 
reference to paragraphs 2.1.7.10 to 2.1.7.13) 
• conduct of SMHA meetings and supervision of MHEs as mandated, deployment of staffs 
in SMHA as per scale and preparation of annual reports/calendars to strengthen the 
monitoring mechanism. (with reference to paragraphs 2.1.8.1 to 2.1.8.4)

Higher Education Department 
2.2  Audit on “Functioning of Jiwaji University, Gwalior” 
2.2.1 Introduction 
Jiwaji University (University), Gwalior was established in May 1964  and is governed by 
Madhya Pradesh Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam 1973 (Act). The jurisdiction of the University 
extends across eight districts57 of the State. It comprises 12 major faculties and 34 academic 
departments, offering academic programs inclusive of 44 postgraduate (PG)  courses, 
21 undergraduate (UG) courses , and five diploma/certificate courses . As of March 2024, 
449 colleges (including 77 government colleges) are affiliated with the University. Besides, 
the Centre for Distance Education offers two UG and five PG courses.  
2.2.1.1 Profile and Organizational Set-up of the University 
The functioning of universities is controlled by the Higher Education Department (HED) 
headed by the Additional Chief Secretary (ACS) at State  level. The Governor of Madhya 
Pradesh is the Kuladhipati (Chancellor) of the University, who appoints the Kulpati (Vice-
Chancellor). Main functions and control of the University are given in Chart 2.2.1 and Chart 
2.2.2. 
Chart 2.2.1: Functions of University Chart 2.2.2: Control of University

Higher Education Department, Government of Madhya Pradesh, three districts Guna, Ashok Nagar and 
Shivpuri came under the Jurisdiction of newly formed Krantiveer Tantya Tope University, Guna.

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit

During the academic session 2023-24, 3,661 and 1.16 lakh  students were enrolled in the 
departments of the University and affiliated colleges respectively. The National Assessment 
and Accreditation Council (NAAC) accredited (April 2023) the University with A++ grade. 
The University was onboarded to the Samarth portal, an d its operations commenced on 
1 October 2025.  
2.2.1.2  Audit Scope and Methodology  
Audit of the University is conducted under the provisions of the Comptroller and Auditor 
General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 . We covered the period 
2021-22 to 2023-24 to assess compliance with the norms prescribed in University Financial 
Code (UFC), University Grants Commission (UGC), Statutes/Ordinances 58 of the 
University and Madhya Pradesh Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam 1973. The audit examination 
included records maintained in the offices of the ACS and Commissioner, HED, as well as 
2259 out of 77 Government Colleges and nine out of 34 University Teaching Departments 
(UTDs) selected through sampling60. Audit of non-government colleges was not undertaken, 
as the Hon’ble High Court, Division Bench, Gwalior had ordered a stay (September 2024) 
on their audit. Details of the selected UTDs and Government Colleges are provided in 
Appendix-2.2.1. 
The University had granted affiliation to 80 colleges61 during 2021-24, out of which records 
of 40 affiliations62 as provided to audit by the University, were scrutinized (August 2025). 
A beneficiary survey 63 was also conducted, covering 220 students from selected colleges 
and 84 students from selected UTDs and their inputs have been suitably incorporated in this 
Report. 
An entry conference was held on 31 July 2024 with Additional Chief Secretary (ACS) and 
Commissioner, HED, wherein Audit Objectives, Methodology, Scope and Criteria of Audit 
were discussed. Audit findings were sent to the Government on 9 June 2025. Exit conference 
was held with ACS, HED on 31 July 2025 and a separate meeting was held with the Registrar 
to discuss the audit observations related to affiliation on 1 September 2025. No separate 
replies of audit findings have been submitted by the Government, however, its responses in 
exit conference and replies of the University, furnished time to time and in meeting, have 
been incorporated suitably in this report. 
2.2.2  Planning, execution and monitoring of academic activities  
2.2.2.1  Affiliation of colleges 
University granted affiliation to 449 colleges in eight districts of the State as of March 2024. 
Process for granting affiliation to colleges is given in Chart 2.2.3 below:
