The records of granting affiliation to 40 colleges64 (Appendix-2.2.2) during audit period, as 
maintained at Jiwaji University, were verified and following shortcomings were noticed: 
2.2.2.1 (i) Inspections of affiliated colleges 
Statute 27 stipulates that college shall be inspected by a Committee 65 nominated by the  
Vice-Chancellor (VC). The Director, College Development Council (DCDC)/Professor 
nominated by the VC will act as Chairperson who shall submit the inspection report to the 
University in the prescribed format, duly signed by all members. 
During scrutiny (August 2025) of records maintained in University, it was observed that no 
Committee was constituted by the University for conduct of such inspections during 
2021-24. Although no formal Committee was constituted, 49 inspections 66 were conducted 
in 30 private colleges by three to four members in each case, during the period. Further, the 
designations of these members were not specified in 28 inspection reports 67 and the 
inspection reports did not state the subject expertise of the members, raising concerns about 
the adequacy and credibility of the inspections. The details are given in Appendix-2.2.3.  
It was also observed that the inspection of nine government colleges  and one private 
college68 had not been conducted so far. Thus, the inspection of colleges was not conducted 
as required under Statute 27. 
While accepting the facts (September 2025) , the University stated that during 2023 -24, 
efforts were made to constitute an Inspection Committee; however, the formal order for its

The reply confirms that inspections of above colleges were conducted without formation of 
Inspection Committee as per norms.  
2.2.2.1(ii)  Shortage of land area and other basic amenities in test checked 
Colleges 
Statute 27  of the University provides that for admission of colleges/institutions to the 
privileges of the University requires that the society 69 applying for college affiliation must 
have at least two acres of land if located in an urban area and five acres if located in a rural 
area. They must also have a constructed building area of 10,000 Square Feet, along with 
other prescribed basic facilities. 
During scrutiny (August 2025) of 31 affiliation records 70 of private colleges, Audit noticed 
shortfalls in the minimum prescribed requirements, including land area, library books, 
laboratories with equipment, and facilities for sports, canteen, health care, toilets, parking, 
as well as safety and security measures, as detailed in Appendices-2.2.4 and 2.2.5.  
While accepting the facts and figures, the University stated (September 2025) that since 
2024-25, affiliated colleges have been directed to address identified infrastructural 
deficiencies. Accordingly, during the 2025 -26 session, courses were curtailed in colleges 
where such deficiencies remained unresolved. It was also informed that, instructions were 
issued to maintain certified records of land and building since 2025-26 onwards and ensured 
to take action against the colleges, deficiencies of which have been pointed out by the Audit.  
2.2.2.1 (iii) Appointment of Teachers and Principal  
Statute 28 stipulates that Principal and Teachers in private colleges shall be appointed by 
the Selection Committee constituted with prescribed members71. 
Audit scrutinized the records related to the appointment of Principals and teachers submitted 
by the applicant private colleges for affiliation and noticed that in 23 colleges, the 
appointments of Teachers and Principals were made by the Management Committ ee of 
colleges instead of Committee as prescribed in the Statute. Whereas in six colleges 72, only 
18 out of 75 teachers were appointed through the prescribed committee as per the provision 
of the Statute. Records of two colleges 73 were not provided. However, all of these colleges 
were granted affiliation despite violating the above mentioned provisions.

While accepting the facts and figures, the University stated (September 2025) that affiliated

Statute 28(17). It was further informed that punitive action has been initiated against 
defaulting colleges; but, the matter is presently sub -judice du e to a stay granted by the 
Hon’ble Court.  
The reply confirmed  that Teachers and Principal s were appointed by Management 
Committee of the Colleges in violation of Statute. 
2.2.2.1 (iv) Affiliation not granted in prescribed time 
Statute 27 stipulates that the University shall process the report of the Inspection Committee 
through its appropriate Bodies and decide to grant, or not to grant, temporary affiliation to 
the college, recording the reasons in writing for its decision within three m onths of 
inspection.  
Audit noticed that out of 31 colleges, Maa Trimukha Law College, Bhind was granted 
affiliation within prescribed timeline, 29 colleges were granted affiliation with delay ranging 
between 49 and 417 days, whereas timeliness in granting affiliation of one college74 was not 
forthcoming from the records furnished to audit. Delay in granting affiliation disrupts 
academic activities, affects college operations, and creates uncertainty for students.  
Year wise details in grant of affiliation of private colleges by University are given in  
Table-2.2.1 below and college-wise details are given in Appendix-2.2.6. 
Table 2.2.1: Year wise grant of affiliation with delay to private colleges  
Year No. of colleges Range of delay after 3 month of inspection of colleges  
(in days) 
2021-22 11 49 to 417 
2022-23 7 99 to 100 
2023-24 23 72 to 84 
Source: Records produced to audit  
While accepting the facts and figures, the University stated (September 2025) that the delay 
occurred due to non -submission of sufficient documents by the respective colleges. 
However, efforts were made to issue affiliations within the stipulated time, and from  
2025-26 onwards, affiliation to colleges has been granted strictly in accordance with the 
prescribed schedule.  
The reply is not acceptable, as delays due to incomplete documentation are attributable to 
the colleges, but the university was required to reject applications that do not comply with 
the prescribed timelines, to maintain procedural discipline and academic schedules.  
2.2.2.1 (v)   Granting conditional affiliation  
Statute 27 states that the University is required to communicate the applicant college about 
identified deficiencies , if any , before finalization of affiliation. However, in any case 
conditional affiliation shall not be granted after first year of the course.  
 
74  Information of City Public College, Ashoknagar  

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
45 | P a g e  
Audit noticed that in 25 cases, the University repeatedly granted conditional affiliation based 
on inspection committee reports during 2021-24, violating the provisions of Statute. Details 
are given in Appendix-2.2.7.  
While accepting the facts and figures, the University stated (September 2025) that the matter 
would be verified, and ensured to take necessary action to avoid such instances in future. 
2.2.2.1 (vi)  Constitution of the Governing Body 
Statute 27 stipulates that in case of an Institution not maintained or managed by Government, 
the application shall also be accompanied by an undertaking that within three months of 
admission of institutions to the privileges of the University, the Institution or College shall  
be controlled by the Governing Body constituted in accordance with the provision of the 
Statute 28.  
Audit observed that the University had neither ensured the formation of Governing Bodies 
in the 31 private colleges 75 nor obtained any undertaking from them, regarding their 
constitution. Granting affiliation without constituting Governing Bodies undermines 
institutional governance and accountability, leading to weak academic and administrative 
control in the affiliated colleges.  
While accepting the facts and figures, the University ensured (September 2025 ) that 
instructions would be issued for the constitution of Governing Bodies in the colleges. 
2.2.2.2  Academic activities 
2.2.2.2 (i)  Enrolment of students in University 
UGC and HED directives (April 2022) provide that state universities are required to adopt 
the Common University Entrance Test (CUET) 76 for UG admissions starting from the  
2022-23 session.  
It was observed that even though the University adopted CUET for the academic session 
2022-23, it did not follow CUET for admissions of 833 students (regular -284 and self 
finance-549) in academic session 2023 -24. Not admitting students through CUET 
undermines the uniform admission process and may affect transparency and merit -based 
selection. 
The University replied (August 2025) that most of the academic courses were non-entrance 
based. Admission in these courses was made on the basis of educational q ualifications. It 
was further assured that admissions in future would be made in compliance with CUET. 
Reply is not acceptable as the objective of CUET to provide equal opportunities  to all 
students and a unified, fair, and transparent admission process in the University was defeated 
during 2023-24.  
 
 
75  of which affiliation records were scrutinized.  
76  A national level exam for admission to undergraduate and postgraduate programs in central and other 
participating universities in India. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
46 | P a g e  
2.2.2.2 (ii)  Deficiencies in teaching records 
The National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 promotes holistic, learner-centered, and flexible 
education with emphasis on experiential learning and timely syllabus completion. To 
implement NEP 2020, the HED directed to conduct lectures for a minimum duration of one 
hour. Further, under the Prachaarya Margdarshika 77, teachers are required to maintain a 
teaching diary to monitor and ensure syllabus completion. 
It was observed that the colleges did not adhere to deliver lectures for prescribed duration 
of minimum one hour, as detailed in Appendix-2.2.8. Additionally, the teaching diaries 
lacked uniform formats and essential details like subject paper, time, and number of lectures, 
making it difficult to verify whether classes were conducted as per the timetable. The 
adverse effect of non-maintenance of teaching diaries is illustrated in Case Study -2.2.1 
below:  
Case Study 2.2.1: Non-maintenance of teaching diary and attendance records of 
students 
In Government College Basai, Datia, 13 guest faculty members were appointed to teach 
45 students (36 in Arts stream and nine in Science stream)  enrolled in the academic session 
2023-24. Teachers did not maintain daily attendance of students and teaching diaries. Despite this, 
₹34.78 lakh78 was paid as honorarium from August 2023 to June 2024.  In absence of teaching 
diary of guest faculty, the basis on which the college calculated monthly payment to the guest 
faculty, could not be determined.  
The In-charge Principal also did not obtain confirmation of syllabus completion and even though 
there was no admission in Commerce stream,  honorarium of ₹6.96 lakh (September 2023 to 
July 2024) was paid to two guest faculties,  raising concerns over the judiciousness of the 
expenditure. 
The shortfall in the delivery of lectures in colleges was reflected in the below -average 
feedback provided by students in the beneficiary survey, indicating gaps in syllabus 
coverage and teaching effectiveness. It was seen  that 15 to 43 per cent students of UTDs 
and test-checked colleges reported the subject knowledge, teaching skill, teaching and doubt 
clearing attitude of teachers and completion of syllabus, as average or below average.  
The Principals of four colleges 79 attributed ( December 2024  and January 2025 ) these 
shortfalls to lack  of classrooms, shortage of teachers , involvement of teachers in non -
academic duties (e.g., elections, college programs), etc. Further, in exit conference, ACS 
directed (July 2025) Officer on Special Duty, HED to issue necessary instructions to address 
and rectify these deficiencies.

2.2.2.3  Conduct and Management of Law Courses in Legal Education Centres  
Ordinance of Bachelor of Law (LLB), regulates admissions, examinations, and related 
matters for the three-years program in affiliated colleges under the University. The semester 
system was followed in these courses as per Bar Council of India (BCI) norms.  
It was seen that Law courses were offered in four out of 22 test-checked colleges. Five-year 
integrated double degree course under self -finance mode was conducted in one college 80, 
and three-year LLB courses under the regular mode were conducted in the three colleges 81. 
The deficiencies noticed in test-checked colleges are discussed in succeeding paragraph: 
2.2.2.3 (i)  Approval of Bar Council of India 
Approval from the Bar Council of India (BCI) is mandatory for running law courses. As per 
directives of the University, colleges were to admit students only after obtaining BCI 
approval.  
It was seen that the required approvals from the BCI for running law courses were not 
obtained before commencement of the sessions or the final admission dates , as depicted in 
the Table-2.2.2 given below: 
Table 2.2.2: Details of approval of Law courses by BCI

It was also observed that the mandatory approval of the BCI for the courses was pending in 
three colleges located in Datia, Gwalior82 and Shivpuri at the time of audit  (March 2025). 
Nevertheless, 60 students were admitted in the college of Datia district during the academic 
session 2022 -23, and 238 students were admitted across the three colleges during the 
academic session 2023-24, without obtaining BCI approval.  
Admitting students in anticipation of obtaining post approval of BCI, not only jeopardizes 
the academic and professional future of the students enrolled but also indicates 
non-compliance with regulatory norms and weak oversight by the affiliating university.  
In exit conference, ACS directed (July 2025) the Officer on Special Duty, HED for conduct 
of an investigation of these irregular admissions.

2.2.2.5 Institute of Distance Education  
The Institute of Distance Education was established (1995) to facilitate distance learning at 
the University. Distance education was offered through two undergraduate and five 
postgraduate courses92, for which 59793 students were enrolled during 2021-24. As per UGC 
guidelines, self-learning materials should be provided to students.  
Audit noticed the following deficiencies in running of the Institute of Distance Education. 
• It was observed that the university conducted examinations for first -year students 
admitted in 2021 -22 and 2022 -23, but did not conduct examinations for second -year 
undergraduate students or final -year postgraduate students. Further, as of December 
2024, examinations for UG and PG courses of the academic year  2023-24 had not been 
conducted. The reasons for non-conduct of examinations were not intimated to audit. 
Due to non-conduct of examination, the students were not promoted to higher classes.  
• It was also observed that the examinations were not conducted as per the scheduled 
timeline. The exams for the academic year 2021-22, originally scheduled for March 2022, 
were held in May/June 2023 . Similarly, the 2022-23 exams, scheduled for March 2023, 
were conducted in January 2024.  
• The University did not ensure to hold required 10 offline classes for B. Com students 
(128 to 255 students) during 2021 -22 to 2023 -24 and did not print or provide  study 
materials in compliance with the instructions of UGC. 
In exit conference, ACS instructed ( July 2025 ) the University to ensure the effective 
functioning of the Institute of Distance Education and timely provision of study material to 
enrolled students. He also emphasized the need to conduct examination in a timely manner 
to fulfill the objectives of the programme.

without ensuring quality

An online tender was invited (November 2022) by the University for procurement of 20 lakh 
Non-OMR answer books and for other printing related works. As per specifications, the 
inner pages and outer pages were to have 75 -80 per cent brightness, while inner pages and 
outer pages were to be of 60 and 105 GSM respectively.

2.2.2.6 (ii) Conduct of Examination  
Ordinance-5 of the University  envisages that the Superintendent of the Examination shall, 
whenever necessary, send a confidential report to the Controller of Examinations about the 
conduct of examination, mentioning therein the performance of the invigilators and the 
general behaviour of the examinees.  
The Superintendent of the Examination at each Centre is personally responsible for the safe 
custody of question papers and the answer-books sent to him and is required to render to the 
University office a complete account of used and unused question papers and answer-books.  
The question papers must be kept in safe custody of the concerned Police Station/ Treasury 
as per instructions of the University, if required.  
Further, the Department (HED) issued an academic calendar for each academic session 
2021-2497, outlining the scheduled timelines for the conduct of examinations and declaration 
of results.

2.2.2.6 (iii) Issue of Degrees to the students 
As per directions of Executive C ouncil (June 2008), the final degree to students was to be 
issued after convocation or Basant Panchami.  Further, the HED directed (June 2023), to 
upload mark sheets and degree certificates to DigiLocker within seven days of result 
declaration.  
It was seen that the University had not implemented the facility for uploading degree 
certificates to DigiLocker. Out of 2.43 lakh pass out students, degree was issued to 
14,870 students during 2021-24. The remaining students were not issued their degrees due 
to non-receipt of applications from them.  
The Registrar replied (December 2024) that degrees were issued only to the students who 
submitted applications, and efforts were being taken to implement the DigiLocker system 
for degree distribution.  
The fact remains that action to upload mark sheets and degree certificates to DigiLocker is 
pending for implementation.  
2.2.2.7  University fellowship 
Ordinance 9 outlines the provisions governing the award of Fellowships, Research 
Scholarships, and other scholarships. As per it, the selection for University fellowship is 
required to be made by a four-member committee, with the Vice-Chancellor or its nominee 
serving as the Chairperson. 
It was observed that a combined committee was constituted in May 2024 for the academic 
years 2023-24 and 2024-25, and applications were invited only for the year 2024-25, thereby 
depriving eligible students of 2023-24 from applying for fellowship benefits. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
54 | P a g e  
The Registrar replied (December 2024) that the committee recommended for inviting 
application for 2024-25.  
The fact remains that due to the delay in constituting the committee for the award of 
fellowships for the academic year 2023-24, eligible students were deprived of their entitled 
benefits. 
2.2.2.8 Student insurance 
The HED periodically instructed the Universities, to ensure that all newly admitted students 
are covered under a Group Life Insurance scheme and to collect the corresponding insurance 
fee at the time of admission.  
It was noticed that the University did not ensure the provision of student life insurance at 
the economical cost and charged ₹100 to each student for insurance coverage under  Star 
Health and Allied Insurance Company Ltd. during academic sessions 2021-22 and 2023-24. 
Further, we noticed that 4,265 students of academic session 2022-23, were not covered under 
insurance. Besides, the University incurred expenditure of ₹50 on account of insurance  
coverage of students against its collection of ₹100 from each student for the purpose.   
In test checked 22 colleges, it was seen that 12 colleges did not insure 1.02 lakh students 
during the period  and nine colleges, despite paying ₹1.44 lakh in premiums for 32,798 
students, did not receive insurance policy from the respective insurance companies , as 
detailed in Appendix-2.2.10. 
Thus, the University and test checked colleges did not ensure to safeguard student’s welfare 
and financial protection.  
In exit conference, ACS advised the University ( July 2025) that insurance agencies should 
be selected through a competitive bidding process and that all students must be brought 
under insurance coverage.  
2.2.2.9  Functioning of Placement Cell 
As per UGC norms, there should be a placement cell in the University.  The cell's primary 
goal is to streamline the recruitment process and to help students in finding suitable job 
roles.  
It was seen that the placement cell in the University was non -functional. Although a nodal 
officer was appointed, the cell was not in existence . Further, the University had framed a 
placement policy, which remained to be implemented due to the inactive status of the cell, 
rendering the intended objectives of the placement cell unfulfilled . However, audit found 
that nine colleges, through campus selection drives, facilitated the placement of 874 students 
during 2021 -24, indicating a positive initiative towards enhancing employment 
opportunities for students in these colleges.  
Registrar replied (December 2024) that action would be taken for functioning of placement 
cell.  
 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit

Case Study-2.2.2: Irregularities in sanctioning and filling of posts  
(a) Government College Pichhore, Shivpuri 
As per HED, GoMP order (March 2018), two teaching posts were to be sanctioned for each 
subject in the Science faculty of undergraduate courses. The college, offering UG courses in 13 
subjects and PG courses in 10 subjects , faced acute shortage of teachers, particularly in the 
Science faculty where only one post was sanctioned for each of four subjects instead of the 
prescribed two. During the audit period, 11 out of 23 sanctioned posts remained vacant and were 
temporarily filled through guest faculties. However, subject -wise deployment was not ensured 
by the college.  
These irregularities in sanctioning and filling of posts resulted in a shortfall in Teacher -Student 
ratio ranging from 1:165 to 1:276 in Arts faculty and 1:61 to 1:81 in Science faculty against the 
UGC norms of 1:30 and 1:25 respectively. Further, in History, a single teacher handled both UG 
and PG classes with 418 to 1,448 students, indicating severe faculty shortage during the period.  
(b) Government college Badouda, Sheopur 
As per syllabus prescribed (September 2021) by HED under National Education Policy 2020, 90 
lectures per paper is required to complete the UG courses. It was seen that UG courses in Arts and 
Science streams commenced in the college from the academic session 2023-24. The Arts stream 
offered seven subjects with seven sanctioned teaching posts, while the Science stream had six 
subjects with 12 sanctioned posts . Since, there were no regular teachers posted , as an internal 
arrangement, 15 teachers from two other colleges of district were arranged to teach Arts subjects, 
and 10 such teachers were arranged to teach five Science subjects. 
Audit observed that shortfall in filling of teaching posts in college, resulted in inadequate delivery 
of lectures ranged from 12 to 37 per paper, which was far below the prescribed norms of 90 per 
paper. Further, during beneficiary survey of 10 students in the college, five students said that 
syllabus was not completed.  
The Principal accepted (January 2025) the shortfall in vacancies leading to inadequate coverage 
of syllabus.

2.2.3.2  Estate management and infrastructure  
2.2.3.2 (i)  Encroachment of university land  
As per the provision in statute no. 21 of the University, subject to the control of Kulpati the 
Finance Officer of the university is responsible for holding and managing the property of 
the University. 
During conduct of land demarcation 105 (May 2005), it was identified that a private 
colonizer106 had illegally encroached University land for developing 48 residential plots. 
The cited land was not having any boundary wall. In its response, against the demarcation, 
the colonizer filed legal cases against the University in the courts of the Additional Collector 
and Additional Commissioner, Gwalior Division. Both the courts delivered judgments in 
favour of the University, in July 2007 and May 2010, respectively. The colonizer escalated 
the matter to the Board of Revenue, Gwalior, which directed for conduct of a fresh 
demarcation in April 2012 in the presence of neighbouring landowners.  
Accordingly, demarcation team constituted in April 2016, submitted its report  (May 2017) 
stating that approximately 6715.86 Square Meter of land107, comprising 65 residential plots 
and valued of around  ₹16.12 crore, were found to be illegally occupied by the colonizer.  
Thereafter, the University brought the issue of encroachment to the attention of the District 
Collector on multiple occasions, but action remained pending as of November 2024.  
In exit conference, ACS advised ( July 2025) the Vice Chancellor to treat the matter with 
utmost seriousness and directed the submission of a detailed report outlining the efforts 
made to reclaim the encroached land. 
The fact remains that, due to non -construction of the boundary wall, University land 
measuring 6715.86 Sq. M. was encroached upon by the colonizer.  
2.2.3.2 (ii) Management of hostels and adequacy of infrastructure 
As per Rule 5 (a) of Govt. colleges hostel rules 1976 , the post of warden/Assistant Warden 
is required to be sanctioned by the Government. Further Rule 10 of ibid Rules provides basic 
amenities and procedure for managing the hostels attached to government colleges. As per 
Bar Council of India (BCI) norms, law colleges are required to have essential infrastructure 
facilities108. 
Further, the Madhya Pradesh  Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam , 1973, provides for the 
establishment, maintenance, and management of colleges, teaching departments, institutions 
of research or specialised studies, laboratories, libraries, museums, and halls.

rooms, moot court room, separate common rooms for male and female students, a legal aid clinic, a well-

need for facility improvements. The inadequacy of infrastructure facilities is illustrated in 
Case Study-2.2.3 given below:  
Case Study-2.2.3: Inadequacy of infrastructure facilities  
(a) Absence of Laboratory for practical in Govt. SMS college Kolaras, Shivpuri 
The college initially began with the Arts stream . The Science and Commerce streams introduced 
in June 2018, were accommodated in the same building without upgradation of infrastructure.  
No dedicated laboratory rooms existed for conducting science practical. The Lab equipment was 
stored in unsuitable rooms with inadequate space for practical use . The computer lab was not 
available, and computers were not procured, making it difficult to conduct practical for Computer 
Science. The students110 of science courses suffered due to lack of basic laboratory. 
Principal replied (January 2025) that there was no science laboratory and practical was conducted 
in classroom. Computer practical were carried out using the laptop/computer of computer teacher. 
The fact remains that college lacked basic infrastructure to run Science and Commerce streams.  
(b) Government College Karahal, Sheopur 
The college was running in the building of Government Model Higher Secondary School Karahal 
since 2018 -19. Three rooms were allotted for the college, one for the Principal  and two for 
classroom. As per the college timetable, three B.A. classes (1st to 3rd year) of seven subjects111 were 
scheduled simultaneously, making it impractical to conduct lectures for all classes with two 
available rooms. 
Additionally, no separate room was available for practical work  required for the subjects of 
Geography. Classrooms also lacked basic facilities, such as adequate furniture and blackboards , 
making teaching conditions substandard. 
The Principal replied (January 2025) that school’s furniture was used during examinations.  
The reply is silent about management of tutorial or practical classes.  
(c) Government Law College, Shivpuri  
Separate setup as per BCI norms was made (August 2013) by HED for functioning of law colleges. 
The HED accorded (September 2022) administrative sanction of ₹7.60 crore for the construction 
of a dedicated law college building in Shivpuri, and six Hectares of land was allotted (May 2023) 
for the purpose. Audit observed that the construction work had not been commenced (February 
2025). In the absence of own building, law courses were being conducted within the premises of 
Govt. Shrimant Madhavrao Scindia Post Graduate College, Shivpuri, alongside other academic 
programs. 
Although the HED designated (February 2024) the Principal of Govt. Law College, Shivpuri, as 
the Head of Office, a separate Centre for Legal Education had not been established in the district. 
Audit observed that, due to the absence of an independent building, critical infrastructure such as 
a dedicated academic block, halls of residence, a separate library building with requisite facilities, 
and a computer lab for law students were lacked. While rooms had been earmarked for the Legal 
Aid Centre and Moot Court112, their condition was found to be sub-optimal.

111  Economics, English, Geography, Hindi, History, Political Science and Sociology  
112  a simulated court where students practice arguing legal cases for educational purposes

2.2.4   Financial Management 
The University received grants from the Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP)  and 
Government of India (GoI) . Apart from this, fee collected from the students admitted, 
affiliation fee collected from colleges, rent to be received from hostels and interest earned 
on deposit are sources of revenue of the University. Affiliation fee received by the University 
was b etween 4.84 and 6.17 per cent  of total receipts during audit period 2021 -22 to  
2023-24. Further, the University did not receive any research grant during the period.   
Expenditure is incurred for salary and allowances, establishment, examination, construction 
and maintenance etc.  
2.2.4.1 Budgeted and Actual Income and Expenditure

period is shown in the Chart-2.2.6 and Chart-2.2.7 below:

expenditure varied from 44 to 54 per cent. Further, the actual income of the University 
was consistently higher than the expenditure. However, it was observed that due to 
decline in receipt of fees and others 113, the income of the University decreased by 
₹ 24.24 crore in 2022 -23. Whereas, the expenditure of the University was increased 
during 2023-24 by 30 per cent due to increase of expenditure on examination and printing 
work, general and other expenses; and capital expenditure.

• A total of 15 departments or sections did not submit their budgetary demands in the year 
2021-22. This number increased to 21 in 2022-23 and further increased to 25 in 2023-24, 
The reasons for non-submission of the budgetary demands by the aforesaid departments 
were not intimated by the University. Thus, the preparation of budget estimates without 
obtaining budgetary demands of income and expenditure from the departments indicates 
unrealistic budget preparation of the University.  
• University did not utilise the revised outlay of ₹31.08 crore 114 against original outlay of   
₹ 89.10 crore 115 during the period under a few heads  like Salary, Printing, Stationary, 
construction of quarters, maintenance etc. 
• Significant underutilisation of funds was seen under the Non-Plan116 (43 to 47 per cent), 
plan117 (69 to 99 per cent), earmarked scheme (83 to 89 per cent) during the period and 
self-finance (71 per cent) in 2022-23 as shown in Appendix-2.2.16. The main reasons  
included estimation being made on the number of sanctioned posts in place of the actual 
working strength, non-utilization of grant and transfer of amounts to University fund in 
subsequent year (2023-24).  
In exit conference, ACS directed ( July 2025 ) the Registrar to ensure that the budget is 
prepared in consultation with all relevant departments and based on the actual available 
manpower. 
Optimum utilization of funds is required to be ensured by the university.  
2.2.4.2 Preparation of Annual Accounts 
Report on Implementation of Accounting Standards in Educational Institutions of 
Department of Higher Education, Ministry of Human Resource Development, GoI states 
that the financial statements of educational institutions (viz., Balance Sheet and Income and 
Expenditure Account) should be prepared on accrual basis in prescribed formats.  
Section 24 -A of Madhya Pradesh Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam  1973 stipulates that the 
Finance Committee of the University shall perform the functions to get the annual accounts 
and the annual audit of the University completed in time. Statute no. 21  of the university 
provides that the Finance Officer is required to keep watch on the state of investment. 
Further Section 48 states that the accounts of the University shall be audited by the Examiner 
of Local Fund Accounts of the State and the copy of the audited accounts together with the 
audit report shall be submitted by the Executive Council to the State Government, which 
shall be laid on the table of the State Legislative Assembly. 
Annual Accounts of the University were prepared manually and audited by the Examiner of 
Local Fund Accounts up to the year 2023-24. However, the following gaps/deficiencies were 
seen:  
 
114   2021-22 (` 8.80 crore), 2022-23 (` 12.36 crore), and 2023-24 (` 9.92 crore) 
115   2021-22 (` 13.78 crore), 2022-23 (` 36.23 crore) 2023-24 (` 39.09 crore) 
116    Non-plan expenditure includes expenditure on salary, office expenses, construction works and 
maintenance etc. 
117   Plan expenditure includes expenditure related to RUSA, UGC and Ayurvedic Consultancy Centre. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
64 | P a g e  
• The standard formats as required for preparing annual account were not prescribed by 
State Government. University prepared Income and Expenditure statement as Annual 
Accounts which do not consist of Receipt and Payment accounts, Income and 
Expenditure accounts and Balance Sheet. Further, the Balance Sheet of University as 
prepared by the Chartered Accountants, is used for filing Income Tax returns only but 
not submitted to state legislature along with Income and Expenditure statement. 
• Income and Expenditure account of the University was prepared on cash basis instead 
of accrual basis as required. Non -preparation of Income and Expenditure on accrual 
basis and non-transferring its balance to Balance Sheet, lead to unfair presentation of 
financial status of the University.  
• Investments of ₹ 306.31 crore were placed in 29 Fixed Deposit Receipts (FDRs) as of 
March 2024. However, the University did not have a policy for investment of surplus 
funds, to ensure strategic financial decision making. 
In exit conference, ACS directed ( July 2025) the Registrar to ensure timely preparation of 
accounts in accordance with the accrual basis. ACS also advised the University to take 
immediate steps to strengthen its investment policy, as substantial funds are involved and 
investment should be made systematically rather than solely relying on Fixed Deposits.  
The University may ensure implementation of a financial management application using 
standardized forms for accrual-based annual accounts.  
The University may ensure formulation and implementation of a policy for investment of 
surplus funds, to ensure strategic financial decision making. 
2.2.4.3 Non-adjustment of outstanding advances  
Rule 166 to 179 of UFC  stipulates the regulation of adjustment of advances given for 
different purposes to university employees. Ordinance 05 of the university  provides for 
submission of account for advances given for examination. All outstanding advances given 
to the employees of University are to be adjusted before 31 March of the financial year . In 
respect of advances in connection with the conduct of examination, the responsibility for 
maintenance and submission of accounts is of the Centre Superintendent/Principal.  
During scrutiny of records pertaining to advances, following were observed: 
• ₹ 82.40 lakh drawn by 79 employees of the University between January 2019 and 
March 2024 were outstanding as of December 2024. The periodic review of advances 
was not conducted by the University. 
• ₹ 4.55 crore out of ₹ 4.92 crore advanced to 142 examination centres during the period 
remained unadjusted (as of December 2024). Besides, advance of ₹ 0.45 crore given 
to 24 centres prior to 2021-22 also remained to be adjusted.  
In exit conference, Registrar stated ( July 2025) that efforts would be made for adjustment 
of advances timely.  
Reply is not acceptable as the prolonged delay in adjusting earlier advances indicates the 
University’s weakness to ensure timely follow-up and settlement.   

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit

2.2.4.4  Utilization of funds received from Central Agencies 
2.2.4.4 (i)  Completion of World bank assisted project for Centre of Excellence 
(CoE)  
The HED, GoMP sanctioned (November 2019) ₹16.49 crore for the establishment of Centres 
of Excellence (CoE) under eight components 118 in seven departments of University and a 
placement Cell.  The activities were to be completed by December 2020 . The objective of 
CoE is to establish specialized facilities in specific subject areas, and to conduct research in 
areas considered important for academic, commercial and research advancement.  
It was observed that against ₹16.49 crore, HED released ₹ 8.35 crore (51 per cent), out of 
which the university utilized ₹2.40 crore as of December 2024. The remaining amount of 
₹5.95 crore (71 per cent) remained unutilized. The underutilisation of funds was primarily 
due to delays in the procurement process. University did not complete the project despite 
multiple time extensions 119 after lapse of five years. The last extension for completion of 
project was upto March 2025. Further, the following deficiencies are noticed in Audit:  
• ₹ 42.61 lakh was spent against allotment of ₹ 65 lakh in five out of seven UTDs for 
purchase of printed books/journals instead of incurring expenditure on e -library during 
2021-23.  
• Three Advanced Equipment 120 valued at ₹35.20 lakh were procured (September and 
November 2021) for the Zoology Department. However, the logbook showed (December 
2024) that the equipment was not in use since October 2023.  
• ₹ 25.60 lakh spent (September and November 2023) on the procurement of two advanced 
instruments for the Botany Departments, which were not taken into use.  
This indicates deficiencies in planning and utilisation of fund. 
Registrar assured (December 2024) that instruments procured for Zoology Department 
would be used in the upcoming academic session. Further it was informed that instruments 
which were procured  for Botany Department could not be used due to unavailability of 
essential glassware and chemicals.  In exit conference, ACS instructed (July 2025) Officer 
on Special Duty of HED to closely monitor and ensure timely completion of these projects. 
Reply is not acceptable as procurement of equipment without ensuring the availability of 
necessary consumables renders the investment ineffective and delays the intended academic 
and research benefits.   
 
118  e-resource, furniture, infrastructure development, E -library enrichment of library and subscription of 
international journals, participation for national -international conference/national seminar, study tour for 
hands on training for students, visiting faculty of national and international repute, procurement of advance 
instruments 
119   24/08/2021, 02/06/2022, 08/12/2022, 28/04/2023 and 04/09/2023 
120   Equipment was for practical use and storage of experimental kits and chemicals.

2.2.4.4 (ii)  Unutilized fund due to non -implementation of Preventing Diabetes 
Scheme 
Ministry of AYUSH, GoI approved (January 2020) ₹1.50 crore for project Ayurvedic 
interventions in managing Type -II diabetes and dyslipidaemia in Gwalior . The project 
period was three years. The University received (February 2020)  ₹60 lakh as first instalment 
against three instalment and the project was commenced in March 2021. 
It was observed that the University had utilized ₹ 24.34 lakh121 during 2021-24 and ₹35.66 
lakh remained unutilized (December 2024)  with the University. The doctors and 
paramedical staff were engaged between February 2021 and September 2022 and thereafter 
no manpower was engaged. Utilization Certificate for ₹ 23.84 lakh was sent (June 2023) 
against first instalment of ₹ 60 lakh. Due to non-utilization of fund, second instalment was 
not released.  The project activities were not carried out as per approved proposal and the 
project completion period (upto March 2024) expired accordingly. Thus, the intended 
objectives of the project could not be achieved and funds of ₹ 35.66 lakh remained idle.  
The Registrar stated (December 2024) that death of the concerned Principal Investigator (PI) 
as the reason behind non -utilization of funds and informed that unutilized funds are being 
returned to the government.  
The reply is not acceptable since University was required to ensure a backup support for 
continuity of the project execution. 
2.2.4.5  Non-implementation of Defined Contributory Pension Scheme  
The Finance Department instructed ( April and December 2005 ) that all Government 
employees appointed on or after January 1, 2005 shall be covered under the Defined 
Contributory Pension Scheme (DCPS) . Further, directions ( May 2011 ) required each 
employee to be registered with  National Securities Depository Limited (NSDL), Mumbai.  
It was seen that the University collected ₹11.43 crore  as employee and employer 
contributions for 429 employees of University during the period and kept in 429 individual 
joint savings accounts (Employee and Registrar) in a bank in Gwalior. Interest was credited 
at the rate 2.80 to three per cent during the period which was substantially lower than the 
returns provided under DCPS . University did not implement the DCPS, thereby depriving 
employees of the benefits intended under the scheme even after lapse of 20 years since the 
inception of DCPS. The audit assessed tentative loss 122 of ₹1.08 crore to 429 employees 
during 2021-24 due to not depositing of contributions to NSDL. 
 
121  Salary- ₹ 15.00 lakh, medicine  ₹ 3.12 lakh, equipment  ₹4.99 lakh, books- ₹0.83 lakh and miscellaneous 
expenses ₹0.40 lakh. 
122  Loss = difference of total return under DCPS and total interest on savings account. Return on deposit in 
2021-22 is calculated applying tentative rate (8.24 per cent) of return for three years (2021-22), similarly, 
return on deposit in 2022 -23 (10.41 per cent for two years) and 2023 -24 (9.88 per cent for one year) is 
calculated applying by the respective rates . The loss of return for previous years and retired employees 
could not be evaluated as details were not furnished to audit. 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
67 | P a g e  
Further, as per information provided (August 2025) by University, three employees retired, 
four employees expired and one employee resigned from service during 2021-24. The funds 
amounting to ₹1.04 crore contributed by them under the DCPS, were retained by the 
University in FDRs and savings accounts and were not paid (August 2025) to the concerned 
employees or their legal heirs.   
The Registrar replied (December 2024) that action was being taken to bring employees 
under the DCPS and a letter of consent was issued to the Pension  Fund Regulatory and 
Development Authority (PFRDA) in May 2024.  
In exit conference, ACS instructed (July 2025) Officer on Special Duty, HED to verify the 
reasons for the non-implementation of the scheme so far. 
The university should put in place a mechanism to ensure timely transfer of contributions 
to PFRDA to safeguard the employee’s interest.  
2.2.4.6  Reconciliation of Bank Balance  
Reconciliation of banking transactions and cash is an important mechanism to verify bank 
and cash balance.  
University operated 36 bank accounts123 linked to five separate cash books124. There was no 
reconciliation between the two and a difference of ₹7.79 crore  was observed between the 
figures of bank accounts statement and respective cash books as of March 2024.  
A First Information Report (December 2022)  was lodged for fraudulent withdrawals of 
₹14.74 lakh from the bank account125, maintained for Rashtriya Uchchatar Shiksha Abhiyan 
(RUSA) scheme between April and June 2021 . This withdrawal occurred on the basis of 
15 cheques which were either cancelled or not issued by the University.  
Furthermore, the University did not reconcile  fees of ₹233.04 crore  received through MP  
Online Portal126 with the actual receivable amounts based on student admission records 
during the period . Similarly, fees of ₹56.93 crore 127 collected through challan or demand 
drafts during the period was accepted based on bank statements, without reconciliation with 
University records. Consequently, the University accepted the reported amounts without 
verification, creating a risk of short receipts or discrepancies going unnoticed. 
It was also observed that eight out of 22 test checked colleges did not reconcile collected 
fees with their records. Three of these colleges did not have information related to fees due 
and actually received for the period , while other five did not reconcile discrepancies 
amounting to ₹3.10 crore. Further, 14 test checked colleges did not maintain separate ledgers 
for the fees collected via MP Online. 
 
123  33 accounts for university fund, three accounts for self -finance fund and except those maintained for 
DCPS contribution

In exit conference, ACS expressed ( July 2025 ) concern in respect of weakness in the 
working of the University in respect of reconciliation mechanisms and advised the Vice -
Chancellor that reconciliation should be done properly, and a robust system should be 
developed to receive the amount in real -time as well as to veri fy the correctness of the 
amount received from the MP online portal. 
2.2.4.7 Maintenance of caution money account and refund thereof 
As per provision contained in Paragraph 66 of Pracharya Margdarshika, Caution Money 
Deposits collected from students at the time of admission, which are not refunded within 
three years after the student finally leaving the college, shall be considered lapsed deposits. 
A register recording the Caution Money Deposits and refu nds for students each year must 
be maintained. Further, as per the directions (February, 1971) of Finance Department, after 
completion of three years of students finally leaving the college, non -refunded caution 
money was to be credited in Government Account. 
It was seen that the University had not developed any mechanism for suo moto refund of 
caution money after completion of course . The refunds were made by the University on 
receiving applications from the students. The University reported refundable caution money 
as liabilities of ₹ 1.15 crore 128 as of March 2024  in the balance sheet. These were not 
supported by subsidiary records containing details in respect of individual students.  
It was further observed that while the Accounts Section and three selected departments 
maintained a refund register depicting only the details of refunds issued, the corresponding 
records of caution money receipts from the respective students were not available with them. 
Further, based on the information furnished by the 13 test checked colleges 129, it was seen 
that caution money of ₹ 77.05 lakh collected from 0.76 lakh students (as on March 2021130) 
was not deposited in the government account.  
In exit conference, ACS advised ( July 2025 ) Vice -Chancellor to establish a proper 
mechanism to ensure suo moto refund of caution money to students after they pass out from 
the University, without requiring them to apply for it.  
2.2.5 Internal control and Monitoring Mechanism 
2.2.5.1 Absence of internal audit 
The Executive Council of the University decided (August 2011) , to implement an internal 
audit system by establishing an Internal Audit Wing, reports of which will be presented to 
the Vice-Chancellor. 
 
128   Self-finance course ₹ 0.84 crore and regular course ₹ 0.31 crore.

entitled for depositing the caution money as they were established during 2019 -24.

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
69 | P a g e  
It was observed that Internal Audit Wing was not established  in the University . In the 
absence of an internal audit mechanism, the University lacked a structured approach to 
monitor financial activities, thereby limiting its ability to prevent or detect financial 
irregularities as pointed out in Paragraphs 2.2.2.6 (i), 2.2.4.4 (i) and 2.2.4.6. 
Registrar replied (December 2024) that action would be taken to establish internal audit 
mechanism. In exit conference, ACS supported ( July 2025) the establishment of a robust 
internal audit system to strengthen the internal control mechanism.  
The university may ensure that internal audit system is established to strengthen the 
internal control mechanism. 
2.2.5.2 Non-formation of Central Appraisal Committee 
As per paragraph 11(a) of Self Finance Courses Regulation 1997 of the University, a Central 
Appraisal Committee was to be formed to review the performance and examine the state of 
finances of each Self-Finance Courses.  
We, however, noticed that Central Appraisal Committee was not formed. Non-formation of 
the committee indicates that the performance of Self-financing courses was not reviewed 
and examined.  
Registrar stated (December 2024) that directions would be issued to form committee. 
2.2.5.3 Functioning of Academic Planning and Evaluation Board 
Section 30 of MP Vishwavidyalaya Adhiniyam 1973 provides the duties of the Academic 
Planning and Evaluation Board, which included duties to prepare the short term and long 
term plan of the University, and to suggest new acad emic programmes to the Faculties and 
to do academic evaluation of affiliated colleges of the University from time to time.  
It was seen that the Academic Planning and Evaluation Board neither carried out academic 
evaluation of affiliated colleges nor conducted time to time evaluation of the functioning of 
UTDs and Schools of Studies (SoS). Although the University had formulated short-term and 
long-term goals in its meeting (6 March 2018) but did not furnish the achievement against 
the goals set.  
Thus, in the absence of evaluation, the remedial actions for improvement could not be taken 
by the University. 
In exit conference, ACS emphasized (July 2025) the need to strengthen internal controls by 
establishing the Academic Planning and Evaluation Board at University level to conduct 
verification in respect of the affiliated colleges.  
2.2.5.4 Physical verification of Store and Central Library 
University financial Code envisaged  annual physical verification of store. Further, as per 
Library Policy of the University, sample physical verification at intervals of not more than 
three years should be done in case of libraries having more than fifty thousand volumes.  

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
70 | P a g e  
It was seen that the University did not conduct annual physical verification of stores since 
2020-21.  Further, 46 unserviceable/not in use items of ₹ 23.63 lakh were lying in three 
departmental stores  and six vehicles had remained non -functional for one to ten years . 
University did not take follow up action for repairing, auction, or write -off of these unused 
equipment and vehicles. It was also observed that the Central Library of University having 
73,291 print books, had not undergone any physical verification since 2016  as required in 
the Library Policy of the University.  
The Registrar replied (December 2024) that necessary instructions would be issued to 
conduct physical verification of the Central Library . In exit conference, ACS emphasized 
(July 2025) conduct of physical verification of the University Store and Central Library.  
2.2.5.5 Action taken to check ragging  
The objective of UGC Regulation on curbing  the menace of ragging in higher educational 
institutions, 2009, is to root out ragging in all its forms from universities, colleges and other 
educational institutions. As per the provisions of the UGC Regulations, the committees viz. 
Anti ragging committee, Anti ragging squad and monitoring cell on ragging are to be formed 
at university level. Measures prescribed in regulation before admission, on admission and 
after academic session are to be followed by the university. 
The University constituted (October 2023) the Anti -Ragging Committee, Squad ,  
Monitoring Cell and appointed a Nodal Officer  in March 2024 . Essential compliance 
measures as required under UGC Regulations131 were lacking in University and test-checked 
colleges, as summarized in Table-2.2.14 below:

2.2.6  Good Practice 
Besides the above observations on functioning of the University, audit also found some good 
practices, which include providing a toll free number132 to resolve the students’ grievances. 
The website of the University provides significant information regarding Admission & Fee, 
Academic, Research, Student life, Alumni, and notice board. Apart from these, a Diabetic 
Health Camp is being organized each Sun day in the Health Centre, to promote early 
detection, effective management, and increased awareness of diabetes within the 
community. 
2.2.7  Conclusion and Recommendations 
Conclusions: 
Audit of Jiwaji University found significant lapses in governance, compliance, and internal 
control mechanism, reflecting systemic weaknesses in the overall functioning of the 
University.  
Audit observed that the University did not constitute Inspection Committee, yet proceeded 
with the inspection and affiliation of colleges without verifying essential pre conditions such 
as availability of land, infrastructure, amenities, and appointment of qualified teachers and 
Principals as per prescribed norms. Further, delays and irregularities were observed in 
granting affiliation and continuing conditional affiliations in violation of existing rules.  
Law colleges were found functioning without approval from the Bar Council of India (BCI), 
and without obtaining NAAC accreditation, which undermines assurance of academic 
standards. Further, the University did not ensure the availability of adequate teachi ng and 
non-teaching staff, leading to the dependency on engagement of guest and contractual 
faculties.  
The infrastructural deficiencies were also noted, which included inadequate furniture, 
laboratory equipment, and security measures. Key student services such as the uploading of 
mark sheets and degree certificates to DigiLocker, provision of group insurance, placement 
support, and anti-ragging measures were also found to be insufficiently implemented.  
The financial management practices of the University were weak and non -compliant with 
established accounting standards. The University did not ensure preparation of accurate and 
effective budgetary estimates, annual accounts on accrual basis, timely adjust ments of 
outstanding advances, reconciliation of bank accounts, deposit of employee and employer 
contributions to National Securities Depository Limited (NSDL) under Defined 
Contributory Pension Scheme and optimum utilization of funds received from Central  
Agencies for the intended objectives.  
 
132  1800-233-1964 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
72 | P a g e  
The audit further observed absence of a dedicated Internal Audit Wing, non -conduct of 
periodical physical verification of stores and stock, and lack of effective internal checks, all 
of which indicate inadequate internal control and monitoring systems. 
Overall, the audit concludes that the functioning of University, suffers from weak 
institutional governance, lack of adherence to regulatory norms, and ineffective financial 
and administrative control. These deficiencies highlight need for strengthening co mpliance 
mechanism, improving transparency and accountability, and instituting robust internal audit 
and monitoring system to ensure that the University operates efficiently and in conformity 
with statutory and academic standards. 
The provision of a toll -free number for addressing students’ grievances, along with the 
online availability of academic information and the organization of health camps, 
demonstrates the good practices adopted by the University.  
Recommendations: 
University may ensure  
• timely recruitment of teaching and non-teaching staff to reduce over-reliance on guest 
faculties and maintain operational continuity (with reference to Paragraphs 2.2.3.1 (i) 
to (iv)).  
• adequate infrastructure and essential facilities are available in affiliated colleges and 
university, to uphold academic standards (with reference to Paragraph 2.2.3.2 (ii)). 
• implementation of a financial management application using standardized forms for 
accrual-based annual accounts. (with reference to Paragraph 2.2.4.2). 
• formulation and implementation of a policy for investment of surplus funds, to ensure 
strategic financial decision-making. (with reference to Paragraph 2.2.4.2). 
• to put in place a mechanism to ensure timely transfer of contributions to Pension Fund 
Regulatory and Development Authority to safeguard the employee’s interest (with 
reference to Paragraph 2.2.4.5).  
• internal audit system is established to strengthen the internal control mechanism (with 
reference to Paragraph 2.2.5.1).

2.3  Audit of “Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission”  
2.3.1 Introduction       
Government of India (GoI) launched National Rurban Mission (NRuM) in February 2016 with 
objective to provide rural areas with economic development and enhanced enforcement of land 
reforms. The vision of the Mission was for development of a cluster of villages (Rurban Cluster) 
that preserve and nurture the essence of rural community life with focus on equity and 
inclusiveness alongwith the facilities perceived to be essentially urban in nature. The identified 
Rurban cluster 133 was to be implemented as a project, over three years  by integrating and 
converging the implementation of project components134.  
Government of Madhya Pradesh (GoMP) selected 19 growth clusters (11 tribal and eight 
non-tribal), comprising of 167 Gram Panchayats (GPs) (Appendix-2.3.1) for implementation 
of project over a five years’ period . The clusters were selected  in three phases during 
February 2016 to August 2018, as shown in Map-2.3.1 below: 
Map-2.3.1: Selection of 19 growth clusters across three phases 
 
 
133  A 'Rurban cluster' is a cluster of geographically contiguous villages with a population between 25000 to 50000 
in plain and coastal areas and 5000 to 15000 in desert, hilly or tribal areas. 
134  (i) Skill development training linked to economic activities (ii) Agro Processing, Agri Services, Storage and 
Warehousing. (iii) Fully equipped mobile health unit. (iv) Upgrading school/ higher education facilities. 
(v) Sanitation (vi) Provision of piped water supply. (vii) Solid and liquid waste management. (viii) Village 
streets and drains. (ix) Streetlights (x) Inter -village road connectivity. (xi) Public transport. (xii) LPG gas 
connections (xiii) Digital literacy. (xiv) Citizen Service Centres (CSC). (xv) Tourism promotion, (xvi) Sport 
Infrastructure (xvii) Social Welfare (xviii) Social Infrastructure (xix) Rural Housing (xx) Environment 
(xxi) Employment generation and SHG formation.

ranked 22nd among all participating States, which indicates the performance of the state was 
relatively poorly in implementing the Mission.

funds from other schemes, while up to 30 per cent is met through Critical Gap Funding (CGF), 
shared between the Central and State Governments in the ratio of 60:40. The State incurred an 
expenditure of ₹1041.01 crore up to March 2024, of which ₹904.20 crore (86.86 per cent) was 
met through convergence and ₹136.81 crore (13.14 per cent) through the CGF. The details of 
funding and utilization thereof from convergence and CGF are given in Paragraph 2.3.4.1 of

The Department of Economic Affairs (DEA), Ministry of Finance, designated 31 March 2022 
as the sunset date of the Mission and allowed completion of works that had already been 
sanctioned or committed as of that date.  
2.3.1.2  Audit Scope and Methodology  
We audited to derive assurance in respect of (a) planning and implementation of the projects 
and (b) utilization of  Critical Gap Funding (CGF) on service delivery -oriented project 
components. 
The criteria were sourced from the guidelines of Government of India (GoI) for the Mission, 
orders/circulars issued by the State Government, approved Integrated Cluster Action Plan 
(ICAP) and Detailed Project Reports (DPRs). 
We covered a period from 2016 -17 to 2023 -24 in five 135 (three tribal clusters and two 
non-tribal clusters) out of 19 clusters, selected through use of statistical tools 136. Besides 30 
(53 per cent) out of 57 Gram Panchayats (GPs) from selected clusters were covered. The 
coverage included all the projects (275 works 137) of selected GPs. Joint Physical verification 
in respect of selected 140 works for determining the progress of works/projects and beneficiary 
survey involving 330 persons from selected GPs were also carried out.  The audit covered 
Development Commissioner, PRDD and Chief Executive Officers of selected Zila 
Panchayats. Further, the records of Director, Rurban mission under the Development 
Commissioner, PRDD were also reviewed. 
An Entry Conference with the Additional Chief Secretary, PRDD was held in October 2024 
to discuss audit objectives, scope and methodology. The Draft Report was sent to Government 
on 01.07.2025. The response of Exit conference (04.08.2025) and replies (28.08.2025)  of 
Government are suitably included and rebutted in this report. 
2.3.2 Planning  
The guidelines of GoI for the Mission stipulate that State Technical Support Agencies (STSAs) 
will be empanelled by the Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD) of GoI to provide inputs for 
selection of clusters, preparation of Integrated Cluster Action Plans (ICAPs) and Spatial Plans 
and to hand hold the States in these processes. Accordingly, ICAP for each cluster was to be 
prepared by the State Nodal Agency in consultation with the State Government, District 
Collectors/Zila Panchayat and concerned Panchayati Raj Institutions with the participation and 
ownership of all concerned stakeholders. The ICAP was to be base document covering baseline 
studies outlining the requirements of the cluster and the key interventions needed to address 
these needs. The ICAP was also required to incorporate tentative cost for development of the 
cluster and an estimate of resource plan required to meet the cost through convergence of 
various Schemes. Further, Detailed Project Reports (DPRs) outlining the comprehensive design 
 
135  Ratibad (Bhopal), Delakhadi (Chhindwara), Kanhiwada (Seoni), Chhimak (Gwalior) and Gunawad (Dhar).  
136  Probability Proportional to Size and Without Replacement method (PPSWOR). 
137  Comprising of 249 completed, 15 ongoing and 11 cancelled works.

and cost estimates of the project components, in accordance with the norms and requirements 
of the relevant scheme guidelines for convergence, were to be prepared for the implementation 
of ICAPs.  
2.3.2.1 Preparation of Integrated Cluster Action Plan 
The Framework for implementation of ICAP prescribed the ICAP (for a cluster) to have two 
components viz. Socio Economic and Infrastructure Planning Component and Initiation of 
Spatial Planning. The Spatial Planning Component of the ICAP was to be initiated  after the 
selection and delineation of the Rurban Cluster and the process was to be consistent with the 
planning norms as applicable in the concerned Statues of GoMP. Planning area notification is 
a necessary condition for preparing a spatial plan and implementation of the Mission.  
The ICAPs for 19 clusters of the state were prepared in three phases, as summarized in

2.3.2.2 Preparation of Spatial Plan and Detailed Project Reports (DPRs) 
2.3.2.2 (i)  Non-preparation of Spatial Plan  
The Framework for Mission implementation, emphasized the preparation of a Spatial Plan as 
part of the Integrated Cluster Action Plan (ICAP) to ensure planned and coordinated 
development.  
Audit observed that the GoMP decided (April 2021) that the preparation of the Spatial Plan of 
all clusters was to be done through young professionals from School of Planning and 
Architecture, Bhopal (SPA) and experienced officers of the Department, and accordingly 
obtained approval from GoI.  The GoI empanelled (October 2021)  SPA and Maulana Azad 
National Institute of Technology (MANIT) Bhopal to prepare the Spatial Plan. PRDD requested 
 
138  Mohana cluster was declared as a Nagar Parishad on 6 September 2019. Consequently, it became ineligible 
for funding under the Rurban Mission, thereby reducing the number of eligible clusters to 18.   

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
78 | P a g e  
(September 2022) for extension from MoRD for preparation of Spatial plan from December 
2022 to 31 March 2023, due to the exit of young professionals from the project. Thereafter, no 
alternate mechanism has been put in place by the government to prepare the Spatial Plan as of 
31 March 2024.  
Thus, no Spatial Plan was prepared before execution of works under the Mission. Despite this, 
various infrastructure projects were executed without spatial mapping of land use and service 
linkages. Implementation of works without the mandatory Spatial Plan  violated the Mission 
framework and undermined the objective of achieving planned and sustainable development of 
Rurban clusters. The absence of a Spatial Plan created the risk of uncoordinated execution of 
works and overlapping or sub-optimal utilisation of created assets. 
The Government acknowledged (August 2025) the fact of non-preparation of Spatial Plan. 
2.3.2.2 (ii) Preparation of DPRs and expenditure thereof  
The guidelines prescribe that  maximum of 30 per cent of the project cost or ₹ 30 crores for 
plain areas and ₹ 15 crores for tribal areas, whichever is less, was to be provided as Critical Gap 
Funding (CGF) shared between Central and State in the ratio of 60:40, in three installments 
over a period of three years and is credited to the dedicated bank account of the SNA. As per 
GoI letter (20 March 2018), variation between ICAPs and DPRs was acceptable139 within plus 
or minus 20 per cent of the cost approved under ICAP.  
A total of 19 ICAPs were prepared, one for each cluster with components ranging from 12 to 
21, and consisting of total 3,462 works for execution. However, the DPRs were prepared for 
only 2,078 works. The details of CGF provisioned in DPRs of 19 clusters and expenditure 140

the reduction in scope and number of works undermined the intended objectives of the Mission 
and diluted the impact of planned interventions.

2.3.3.2 Shortfalls in implementation of Key Project Components 
Significant delays and non-implementation were noticed in major components of the Mission, 
including agriculture and allied activities, piped water supply to targeted households, 
implementation of smart classes in schools, and works related to social and basic infrastructure. 
These gaps indicate weak planning, delayed execution, and poor monitoring, which adversely 
affected the achievement of intended objectives of the Mission. The status of such components 
is detailed as under: 
2.3.3.2 (i) Non-execution of income generating initiiatives under agriculture and 
allied activities  
As per Mission guidelines, the components pertaining to agriculture and allied activities would 
be required to be given special emphasis while developing the identified clusters. The Mission 
framework thus envisages integrated development of the agricultur al and allied sectors to 
enhance income generation and ensure sustainable economic growth within the clusters. There 
were 83 activities/works (Appendix-2.3.4) classified under this component.  
As shown in Chart-2.3.4, provision for execution of 313 works under the category were 
approved in the state, out of which 150 (47.92 per cent) were completed, 86 (27.48 per cent) 
were in progress, and remaining were not initiated.  
It was observed that under this category, focus was given mostly to activities such as 
construction of check dams, stop dams, and recharge shafts. However, income -generating 
initiatives like Beekeeping and Honey Processing Plants, Dairy Development or Proc essing 
Units, Bulk Milk Chilling Machines, Agri-Processing Units (for flour, pulses, vegetables, fruits, 
honey, etc.), Construction of Indigenous Seed Banks, Vegetable Cultivation Hubs, Collection 
Centres for Vegetables/Milk, and Community -Based Farming we re either excluded from 
selection or selected but not initiated. As a result, implementation under the Mission did not 
achieve its intended objective of promoting income-generating activities. 
In Beneficiary survey also, 62 per cent of respondents stated the above services were not made 
available. Thus, the Mission objective of reducing poverty and generating employment in rural 
areas could not be fulfilled due to non-execution of agricultural services and processing, storage 
and warehousing works under the Mission. 
The Government attributed (August 2025) non-initiation of these activities to land availability 
issues and the premature closure of the Mission, It was further stated that w hile check dams 
were largely completed and beneficial, major infrastructures like agro parks, warehouses, and 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
83 | P a g e  
processing units could not progress due to delays in land allotment, caused by elections and 
Covid-19. Though designs, estimates, and tenders were prepared, most works could not be 
started before the Mission’s closure, with limited progress achieved only i n a few districts 
post-2022.  
The justification of delays due to land issues and elections is not acceptable, as the department 
already assured the availability of land at DPR level and, contingencies should have been 
addressed during planning. Non -completion of key infrastructures des pite preparatory work 
reflects weak planning, and poor execution.  
2.3.3.2 (ii) Piped water supply to targeted households  
National Rural Drinking Water Program (NRDWP) is implemented by the Ministry of Drinking 
Water and Sanitation (MoDWS), GoI to provide every rural person with access to 70 litres per 
capita per day (lpcd) clean drinking water within their household premises or at a horizontal or 
vertical distance of not more than 50 meters from their household without barriers of social or 
financial discrimination by 2022. 
As shown in Chart-2.3.4, 212 works were approved for round the clock water supply in the 
19 clusters in the State. For its execution a provision of CGF of ₹ 73.81 crore was made . Of 
these 40 works (18.87 per cent) were completed, 121 works (57.08 per cent) were in progress, 
and remaining were not initiated.  
Audit found deficiencies in water supply works executed in the selected clusters, except 
Gunawad (Dhar), where water supply was operational, as summarized below: 
🏘️  Ratibad Cluster (Bhopal): ₹ 5.36 crore was spent on 16 works  against sanction of 
₹ 8.69 crore. 14 of these had been completed, one (Barkheda Nathu) was incomplete, 
and one (Kharpa-GP Semri Bazyaft) was cancelled.  
🏘️  Chhimak Cluster  (Gwalior): All the 12 works were shown as completed, with 10 
handed over to the Gram Panchayat. In the remaining two works 142 neither the 
Completion Certificates were issued nor were handed over to the Gram Panchayats. 
🏘️  Delakhedi cluster (Chhindwara): Two overhead tanks of ₹ 17.42 lakh at Bangai and 
₹26.51 lakh at Pratapgarh Badla were completed in January 2021. They were not being 
used for water supply due to water seepage (GP Bangai) and faulty borewell (GP 
Pratapgarh) after four years of completion.  
🏘️  Kanhiwada (Seoni): The Overhead tank constructed was leaking and the railing on it 
was broken suggesting issues of quality in the construction. Despite the two tubewells 
bored, water is being supplied through tankers.  
The above deficiencies in piped water supply indicate that intended water supply benefits to 
households were not ensured, as was also reported by 42 per cent of the beneficiaries who stated 
non-availability of piped water in the beneficiary survey. 
 
142  Gram Panchayat- Maharjpur-1 and Chhimak-1. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
84 | P a g e  
The Government stated (August 2025) that although piped water supply was identified as a key 
demand in all clusters during the ICAPs (2016-18), no funds were provided by GoI to the PHE 
Department, a designated implementing agency. Consequently, proposed works in Acchat 
(Chhatarpur) and Kursa (Singrauli) clusters were cancelled, as the requirement was later taken 
up under Jal Jeevan Mission and other government schemes. 
Cancellation or non -initiation of piped water supply works, despite being identified as a key 
demand, along with non-handing over of completed projects and sub-standard quality of work 
as identified above, reflects weak planning, and inadequate monitoring of works execution.  
2.3.3.2 (iii) Implementation of smart classes in schools  
As envisaged in the Mission framework, education forms one of the components under the 
thematic area of Enabling and Empowering of Individuals. The framework indicates the 
objective of improving access to quality learning and promoting digital literacy wit hin the 
clusters. Further, Rajya Shiksha Kendra, Bhopal directed the Development Commissioner, 
Bhopal, (January 2022) that each smart class in selected Government Middle Schools (Classes 
6 to 8) was to have an LED TV, laptop and an UPS of 1.0 KVA, dedicated trained teacher, 
electricity connection, safe building, insurance, internet facility etc.  
Audit observed that a n administrative sanctions of ₹ 40.63 lakh  were issued for execution of 
35143 works under the category in the state. 18 of these works were suspended, 11 were 
cancelled and six were ongoing after incidence of expenditure of ₹ 13.90 lakh. Thus, none of 
the works relating to establishment of smart classes in schools was completed, in dicating 
ineffective implementation of educational infrastructure initiatives under the Mission and non-
achievement of its objectives of improving the quality of education and promoting mental 
development of targeted children in the clusters.  
The Government stated (August 2025) that  prolonged school closures during the Covid -19 
period led to non -release or return of funds for smart classes, resulting in suspension or 
cancellation of works after Mission closure. Although smart classes were approved in all ICAP 
phases, equipment was pro cured only in seven Phase -I clusters, while activities under Phases 
II and III and part of Phase I were halted, and the unutilised funds were returned to the State 
Nodal Agency. 
The delay in implementing smart classes despite pre -Covid approvals and the return of 
unutilised funds indicate poor planning and weak monitoring of the Mission implementation.  
2.3.3.2 (iv) Incomplete/suspended/cancelled works of social and basic infrastructure  
PRDD issued (September 2021) orders regarding delegation of powers for technical approval 
of new construction works, administrative sanction, financial approval and release of funds, 
transfer of constructed infrastructure, operation and maintenance of infrastructure/projects etc. 
to various officers at the district level and PRIs, etc. Further, no advance was to be given to 
implementing agencies in Public Financial Management System (PFMS) w.e.f. October 2021. 
 
143 Ratibad (Bhopal-12), Simrol (Indore-5), Kanhiwada (Seoni-7) and Achhat (Chhatarpur-11) clusters.

dispensaries, training centers, Anganwadi buildings, boundary walls in schools, haat bazaars, 
parking sheds etc. The shortfalls in execution of these works by the executing agency (Rural 
Engineering Services) indicate gaps in the creation of essential community assets, adversely 
affecting the delivery of social services in the clusters. Further, Audit found deficiencies in 
social and basic infrastructure component works executed in the selected clusters, as 
summarized below:

departmental funds, while funds of the Gunawad  sub-health centre were diverted to another 
facility. Delay in works execution were also attributed to recall of unspent balances and 
transition to PFMS. Additionally, 36 uninitiated works were cancelled at Mission closure 
(31 March 2022), and some others were dropped due to rollout of Jal Jeevan Mission and effect 
of Covid-19. 
The reply attributing delays to PFMS transition, recall of unspent balances, and Mission closure 
is not convincing, as these were predictable administrative aspects that should have been 
managed with proper planning. Further, the cancellation of 36 uninitiated works and diversion 
of funds indicate weak financial management and poor monitoring. Moreover, dropping 
approved works due to parallel schemes like Jal Jeevan Mission highlights inadequate 
coordination to achieve targeted outcomes under the Rurban Mission. 
2.3.3.3  Local Infrastructure and Skill Development Initiatives  
As per approval of ICAPs, CGF of ₹ 405 crore was to be provided for Mission implementation 
in 19 clusters. CGF of ₹177. 75 crore was provided up to 31 March 2022, of which 
₹ 136.81 crore was utilized as of 31 March 2024. GoI closed window for CGF with effect from 
31 March 2022, leading the GoMP to tackle incomplete and non -initiated projects/  
interventions.  
The status of utilization of CGF in five selected clusters included ₹14.47 crore (48.23 per cent) 
out of provision of ₹ 30 crore in Ratibad, Bhopal. Similar, position in respect of Chhimak 
(Gwalior) was ₹12.32 crore (41.0 7 per cent ), Kanhiwada (Seoni) was ₹ 3.39 crore 
(22.60 per cent), Gunwad (Dhar) was ₹ 5.24 crore ( 34.93 per cent ) and ₹ 7.94 crore 
(52.93 per cent) in respect of Delakhedi (Chhindwara) cluster, as can also be seen in 
Chart-2.3.3 of the report.
