common and should have been managed better to avoid slowing down important works. 
2.3.3.4   Service Level Benchmark 
In the Model Planning Guidelines for Rurban Clusters , the GoI suggested setting benchmarks 
for various components in the Mission under different categories. Proposed benchmarks are to 
be achieved within the time frame of the project period as mentioned in the ICAPs or proposed 
Cluster Spatial Development Pl ans, which could range from five to ten years as per State 
Government notification. 
It was observed that no notification in this regard was issued by the State Government. Absence 
of notified service level benchmarks led to lack of uniform standards for evaluating 
performance and measuring outcomes of the Mission components, which in turn  led to 
non-assessment of various components of the Mission under different categories.

2.3.3.5 Operation & Maintenance (O&M) of constructed properties  
SLEC in April 2021 decided that the GPs would enter into a Memorandum of Understanding 
(MoU) with the Village Organization (VO), Cluster Level Federation (CLF) of Self Help 
Groups of the State Rural Livelihood Mission (SRLM) for the maintenance and operation of all 
revenue generating infrastructure. PRDD issued (October 2021 and November 2021) directions 
to CEOs of 15 ZPs (having Rurban clusters) regarding O&M of shops constructed/under 
construction. These shops were to be owned by the PRDD and were to be allotted by a 
Committee147 to CLFs on rotation basis.  
It was seen that under the Mission, 499 works (1 ,074 works for all 19 clusters), at a cost of 
₹38.71 crore were completed between January 2019 and October 2024, in selected clusters 
through various implementing agencies. However, O&M of these constructed properties was 
not ensured even after lapse of four years of instructions issued by PRDD.  
Cluster-wise summary of works148 executed in selected clusters, is shown in below Chart-2.3.7, 
whereas component-wise details are given in Appendix-2.3.5.  
Chart 2.3.7: Summary of assets created in selected clusters  
 
It was observed that in the selected clusters, 18 shops attached to passenger waiting halls and 
community buildings (₹ 82.34 lakh) and three bus stands with 27 shops (₹1.0 6 crore) were 
constructed and handed over to the respective Gram Panchayats  between 05.10.2020 and 
19.07.2024. However, none of the 45 shops were allotted on rent and remained vacant. Further, 
operation and maintenance of the bus stands was not undertaken. The absence of information 
regarding constitution of the management committee, indicates that no Self Help Groups 
(SHGs)/Cluster Level Federations (CLFs)/ Village Organisations (VOs) were engaged for 
operating these assets. Thus, even after four years of completion, neither O&M activities nor 
revenue realisation from rent had been commenced by the GPs, as depict ed in 
Photographs-2.3.5:

concerned and representative of CEO, ZP.

Government stated (August 2025) that some assets have been rented out while the rest would 
be utilized shortly, and that these assets vest with Gram Panchayats under the Panchayat Act, 
1994. Further, once the infrastructure is completed,  it would be handed over to 
SHGs/CLFs/VOs for revenue generation after entering MoU with GPs. 
The reply confirms that instructions for entering MoUs for Operation & Maintenance were not 
followed strictly, which resulted in continued loss of revenue for four years.  
The government may ensure commencement of operation and maintenance activities of the 
revenue-generating projects by enabling proper ownership of the assets so created.   
2.3.3.6 Beneficiary survey 
Basic amenities (Inter-village road connectivity, round the clock piped water supply, Access 
to village streets with drains, Village streetlights, sanitation and public transport) related 
infrastructure works were completed to a limited extent. 
During the beneficiary survey, 86 questions on 23 components were asked to 330 beneficiaries 
from five selected clusters. The percentage of availability and non -availability of service 
delivery was communicated as shown in Chart-2.3.8 below:  
Chart 2.3.8: Summary of responses of the beneficiary survey  
 


Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
93 | P a g e  
The above findings reflect that 51.08 per cent of respondents acknowledged availability of 
service delivery -oriented facilities under the Mission, whereas 48.92 per cent  expressed a 
contrasting view, indicating that nearly half of the intended beneficiaries did not perceive any 
noticeable improvement in access to such services.  
2.3.4 Financial Management 
2.3.4.1 Fund Mobilization and Critical Gap Funding under Rurban Mission 
Funds for the Project were to be mobilized through convergence of various Centrally and State 
Sponsored Schemes in accordance with the relevant Scheme Guidelines, which were to be 
routed through the bank accounts at the State as well as the district level and a maximum of 
30 per cent (sharing between Central and State in ratio of 60:40)  of the project cost was to be 
provided as CGF in three installments. The release of first two installments of 30 per cent each 
were linked to approval of ICAP by GoI and approval of DPRs by SLEC respectively. The State 
Nodal Agency (SNA) for the scheme was to transfer 35 per cent of the CGF received as third 
installment to the Project after submission of Utilization Certificate (UC) and field visit by 
official of GoI and the balance five per cent after completion of construction of all project 
components. 
The details of funding and utilization thereof from convergence and CGF in respect of 19 
Clusters in the three phases are given in Table-2.3.3:

The above shows that expenditure of ₹ 904.20 crore (70 per cent) was incurred against approved 
amount of ₹ 1,292.15 crore under convergence, whereas it was of ₹ 136.81 crore 
(33.78 per cent) against approved CGF component of ₹ 405 crore. Thus, upto  the third phase 
of the scheme, ₹ 1,041.01 crore (61.34 per cent ) was incurred against total sanction of 
₹ 1697.15 crore, of which ₹  904.20 crore (86.86 per cent) was met through convergence and 
₹ 136.81 crore (13.14 per cent) through the CGF. This indicates greater reliance of the State on 
convergence funding, with relativel y limited utilization of CGF resources, resulting in a 
significant portion of the sanctioned funds remaining unspent. Further, it highlights slow 
implementation progress and points to gaps in planning, execution, and monitoring, which 
hindered the timely completion of activities before the scheme’s closure.  

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
94 | P a g e  
Government stated ( August 2025 ) that initially funds were released cluster -wise as per the 
allocated Critical Gap Fund (₹30 crore for non-Tribal and ₹15 crore for Tribal), with the second 
installment released after utilization of 60 per cent of the first. Subsequently, GoI revised the 
process and instructed utilization of funds through a common pool instead of cluster -wise 
allocation. 
The reply is not acceptable as change in fund release procedure does not justify delays and 
unspent balances, which reflect weak financial planning and poor monitoring.  
2.3.4.2 Irregular utilization of CGF funds  
The CGF received for development of clusters was to be transferred to dedicated bank accounts 
at the district level by SNA. These funds were to be utilized for the components of ICAP 
proposed to be funded by the CGF.  
It was seen that  Director Rurban Mission, Bhopal irregularly transferred an amount of 
₹ 30.00 crore to Madhya Pradesh Employment Guarantee Scheme (MPEGS) in February 2018. 
This amount was recouped through two transfers viz. ₹ 24.58 crore   (January 2019) and 
₹ 5.42 crore (March 2019). This irregular transfer indicates irregularities in fund management, 
resulted in a loss of interest of ₹ 78.26 lakh, and affected the timely implementation of scheme 
activities.   
Government stated (August 2025) that no interest was generated as the funds were utilized for 
payment of salaries and honorarium of MGNREGS staff, which was not an irregular utilization. 
The reply is not acceptable as diversion of Mission funds for purposes outside the approved 
activities was observed, underscoring the need for stronger financial discipline. 
2.3.4.3 Non-submission of Utilization Certificate  
The Guidelines prescribe that the third installment of 40 per cent of CGF was to be released 
after submission of Utilization Certificate (UCs) as per General Financial Rules (GFR) norms 
and field visit by the MoRD, GoI. PRDD was to verify the completion of the project by visiting 
the sites. A copy of the completion report was to be sent to the Ministry for information and 
record.   
It was seen  (July 2024) that  ₹ 177.75 crore  was received by the SNA from GoI and GoMP 
under the scheme for project expenditure. Against the project expenditure of ₹ 120.41 crore , 
UCs of ₹ 90.20 crore for the period up to 2020-21 were sent to the GoI, leading to pendency of 
₹ 30.21 crore as of 30 September 2021. The Books of Account of SNA for the years 2021-22 
to 2023-24 were not produced to audit.  Therefore, audit could not ascertain the c urrent status 
of expenditure and outstanding UCs.  
It was also seen that ₹ 6.15 crore (during 2016 -17 to 2020 -21) was spent on administrative 
expenditure against receipt of ₹ 11.08 crore by the SNA. UCs of ₹ 3.14 crore in respect of this 
component were sent to the GoI with pendency of UCs of ₹ 3.01 crore. No further UCs were 
seen to have been submitted to GoI and no funds under the Mission were released as third 
installment.  
Government stated (August 2025) that audited UCs up to 2020-21 were submitted, while UCs 
for 2021-24 are prepared but pending for submission due to delays in receipt of audited UCs 
from districts after delegation of powers in October 2021. 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
95 | P a g e  
Delegation of powers cannot justify delayed submission of UCs, which is a core accountability 
requirement. The pendency reflects weak financial control and inadequate monitoring, 
validating Audit’s observation. Further, the Department did not submit the co pies of UCs 
submitted thereafter and the reply is silent about non-furnished books of accounts to audit.

2.3.5.1 Inadequate monitoring and evaluation by SNA 
As per the guidelines, the scheme was required to have effective Monitoring and Evaluation 
mechanisms in place. Field visits were to be conducted before the release of the 3rd installment 
of CGF. The team was to comprise officials from the Ministry as well as State Nodal Agency. 
No details in respect of any field visit by the State Nodal Agency were provided either by the 
SNA or by the selected ZPs. Therefore, audit could not ascertain the frequency of inspections 
carried out by SNA. However, in response to audit enquiries, ZP Bhopal  and Dhar stated 
(January 2025) that no inspection was carried out at the State level. ZP Chhindwara replied 
(January 2025) that State Level authorities had inspected before release of 3 rd installment, but, 
no records of the visit were made available. Web-based monitoring using Geotagging was also 
communicated as being done.  
The Government stated (August 2025) that monitoring was done by Young Professionals and 
District Collectors, with delays attributed to Rural Engineering Services ( RES) and Public 
Health Engineering ( PHE). Limited SNA manpower until 2020 restricted visits, but after 
additional staff deployment in 2021, some district visits and ACS review meetings were 
conducted to expedite works. 

Report of the C&AG of India on GoMP for the period ended March 2024 
96 | P a g e  
Delayed strengthening of monitoring and over -reliance on limited staff reflect inadequate 
oversight. Weak monitoring contributed to slow progress of works and sub -standard work as 
indicated in Paragraphs 2.3.3.1 and 2.3.5.4. 
Thus, the assessment of targets and achievement of the expected outcome relating to 
development of service delivery-oriented facilities in the rural areas was not done. 
2.3.5.2 Inadequate monitoring by District Level Committee (DLC) 
As per PRDD order (August 2018) the DLCs were formed which were responsible to watch the  
progress of implementation of the mission, release of funds, review of use of funds, field tours, 
establishment of convergence between various sectors and monitor the works. The DLCs were 
required to meet at least once in a quarter.  
Against the required 14 meetings 149, a single meeting150 of the DLC was held in each selected 
cluster, for notification of the cluster as a planning area. In absence of regular meetings of the 
DLC, monitoring of activities under the scheme remained inadequate. 
Government acknowledged (August 2025) that DLC monitoring was inadequate, but review 
and field monitoring were ensured through regular instructions from the State office to district 
authorities. 
2.3.5.3 Control on construction works carried out by various implementing 
agencies  
GoI had entrusted the responsibility of implementation of the Mission to the PRDD. The 
amount to be spent on the work under the Mission was to be spent from the schemes of the 
department concerned and supplemented through CGF component. SLEC decided (December 
2021) that the Zila Panchayat will be responsible for providing records/data for Audit. 
It was seen that 527 works, as detailed in Appendix-2.3.6 (as summarized in Table 2.3.5 below) 
were carried out by the various departments from the CGF component. Funds were provided 
directly to the implementing agencies through Director, Rurban Mission. Consequently, Zila 
Panchayats were not in possession of complete records relating to these works, resulting in 
non-availability of requisite documents for audit verification.

2.3.5.4  Joint Physical verification 
Joint physical verification (JPV) of 140 works executed by GPs was carried out with 
departmental authorities (Details are given in Appendix-2.3.7). It was seen that the six works 
were incomplete. Further, it was seen that 84 works 151 (Appendix-2.3.8) were executed by 
GPs without material testing as was required under PRDD order August 2005. Non-carrying 
out lab test material carries risk of sub -standard quality of construction works . During JPV 
audit also noticed sub -standard quality in 12 works. Photographs of three sites with 
sub-standard works are shown below:

Government stated (August 2025)  that letters were issued to respective districts to explain 
reasons for non-completion and to ensure early completion of works with necessary testing.  
Detailed reply on the above identified issue, was awaited in audit (October 2025). 
2.3.6 Conclusion 
Audit o f Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission found significant deficiencies in 
governance, compliance, and internal controls, indicating underlying inefficiencies in the 
Mission’s implementation across the State.  
Audit observed that although the Mission was discontinued on 31.03.2022, with instructions of 
the Government of India to complete all sanctioned or committed works, 48 per cent of these 
works remained incomplete or uninitiated even after two years of its discontinuation.  
The government executed various infrastructure projects under the Mission, without spatial 
mapping of land use and service linkages, which undermined the objective of achieving planned 
and sustainable development of Rurban clusters.  
Further, the government did not ensure integrated development of the agricultural and allied 
sectors under the Mission, which limited the opportunities for income generation and 
sustainable economic growth within the clusters. Similarly, the objectives suc h as providing 
piped water supply to targeted households and establishing smart classes to provide better 
education and mental development of the targeted children were not achieved as intended. It 
was also observed that even though provisions were made fo r developing the local 
infrastructure and skill, execution remained inadequate, which resulted in gaps between 
intended objectives and actual outcomes.  
Due to not entering Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs) by the concerned Gram Panchayats, 
the revenue-generating assets were not handed over to the Self Help Groups (SHGs), Cluster 
Level Federations (CLFs) or the Village Organizations (VOs), leading to non -commencement 
of their operation and maintenance activities.  
Audit further observed that of the total expenditure incurred by the State, 86. 86 per cent was 
met through convergence and only 13.1 4 per cent  through the Critical Gap Fund (CGF), 
reflecting greater dependence on convergence resources and limited utilisation of CGF funds. 
The inadequate conduct of District Level Committee (DLC) meetings, and not carrying out lab 
testing of the material used in e xecuted works, reflected weak internal control mechanisms in 
Mission’s implementation, as was co rroborated by the findings of Joint Physical Verification 
of works, conducted by the Audit.  
Overall, the audit concludes that the success of Mission in Madhya Pradesh was limited, with 
achievements largely confined to project execution primarily funded through convergence with 
other schemes.   
 
 

Chapter-II: Compliance Audit 
99 | P a g e
 2.3.7  Recommendations 
The government may ensure  
• completion of ongoing projects, which were committed under the Mission ( with
reference to Paragraphs 2.3.3.1 and 2.3.3.2(iv)),
• commencement of operation and maintenance activities of the revenue -generating
projects by enabling proper ownership of the assets so created ( with reference to
Paragraph 2.3.3.5),
• to fix responsibilities for deficient planning, implementation, monitoring and financial
management (with reference to Paragraphs 2.3.2.2, 2.3.3.1, 2.3.4.1, 2.3.5.2 and 2.3.5.3).
